<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the Hellinger conjecture for Boolean functions on the uniform discrete cube, with arbitrary output bias. For a Boolean function of mean <i>m</i> and every $`\rho\in[-1,1]`$, the loss $`\sqrt{1-m^2}-\mathbb E\sqrt{1-(T_\rho f)^2}`$ is at most $`1-\sqrt{1-\rho^2}`$, with equality for signed coordinates. The proof combines asymmetric dimension induction, a calibrated noise-semigroup energy estimate, and finite exact arithmetic certificates. The Hellinger inequality also yields the Courtade–Kumar information bound.

- **claim-hellinger-main** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every $n\ge1$, every Boolean function $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$, and every $\rho\in[-1,1]$, writing $m=\mathbb Ef$, one has $$\label{eq:main} H(m)-\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)\le1-H(\rho).$$ Both expectations are with respect to the uniform probability measure.
  - Explanation: Signed coordinate functions maximize the weighted Hellinger affinity loss under product noise for every Boolean function and every output bias on the uniform cube.
- **claim-induction-range** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-induction)): Let $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$, let $m=\mathbb Ef$, and let $0<\rho<1$. If $s=\sqrt{1-\rho^2}\le .84$, then $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)\ge H(m)-1+s.$$
  - Explanation: In the strong-noise regime the Hellinger lower bound on the noised expectation is proved by compression to increasing functions and dimension induction with an asymmetric profile.
- **claim-compression** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ind-lem-compression)): For every Boolean function $f$ there is an increasing Boolean function $\widetilde f$ with the same mean such that $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho\widetilde f)\le \mathbb EH(T_\rho f).$$
  - Explanation: Sorting each coordinate replaces a Boolean function by an increasing one with the same mean without increasing the expected Hellinger profile of the noised function.
- **claim-small-coordinate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ind-lem-coordinate)): If an increasing Boolean function of mean $m$ has at least four active coordinates, one of them has first-level coefficient $$0<x=\mathbb E[f(X)X_i]\le b(m).$$ The two sections in this coordinate have means $m-x$ and $m+x$.
  - Explanation: An increasing function with enough active coordinates has a coordinate whose first-level coefficient is positive and bounded by the explicit profile of the mean, so both sections have controlled means.
- **claim-scalar-inequalities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar)): For every $0<s\le .84$, choose the parameters and the polynomial $B$ as above. Then $B\ge0$ on $[-1,1]$, $B(0)=1$, $B(\pm1)=0$, and the following four assertions hold.* 1. *For $0\le p_0\le p_1\le1$, write $z=p_1-p_0$ and $p_u=p_0+uz$, and set $$D=\frac{G(p_\delta)+G(p_{1-\delta})-G(p_0)-G(p_1)}2, \qquad S=\alpha G(p_1)+(1-\alpha)G(p_0).$$ Then $D\ge0$ and $$\label{ind:eq:pair} D(S+\Lambda D)\ge \mu s^2\left(z-\frac{G(p_1)-G(p_0)}{4L}\right)^2.$$* 2. *For every $m\in[-1,1]$, $$\label{ind:eq:reflection-profile} \frac{s}{2}\bigl(B(m)+B(-m)\bigr)\ge H(m)-1+s.$$* 3. *For $m\in[-1,1]$ and $0<x\le b(m)$, put $$B_\pm=B(m\pm x),\qquad C=\frac{B_+-B_-}{2x},\qquad A=\frac{2B(m)-B_+-B_-}{2x^2}.$$ Then $$\label{ind:eq:profile} A\bigl[B(m)+(2\alpha-1)xC+(\Lambda-1)x^2A\bigr] \le \mu\left(1-\frac{s}{2L}C\right)^2.$$* 4. *Every increasing Boolean function depending on at most three coordinates satisfies $$\label{ind:eq:base} \mathbb EG\bigl((1+T_\rho f)/2\bigr)\ge sB(\mathbb Ef).$$
  - Explanation: Four certified scalar assertions supply the pair gap bound, the reflected profile comparison, the induction closure condition and the small-dimension base case.
- **claim-asymmetric-induction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric)): For the fixed $s$, parameters, and profile above, every increasing Boolean function satisfies $$\label{ind:eq:all-dimensions} \mathbb EG\bigl((1+T_\rho f)/2\bigr)\ge sB(\mathbb Ef).$$
  - Explanation: The asymmetric profile estimate propagates from few active coordinates to every dimension by averaging a pointwise pair inequality and using monotonicity of the resulting root bound.
- **claim-crossing-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-crossing)): Suppose Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> is known for $s\le .84$. If it fails for some $s>.84$, then for the same function there is a parameter $.84\le s<1$ for which $$\label{eq:crossing} b=h-1+s,\qquad \mathcal E(d)\le K.$$ It is enough to exclude Equation <a href="#eq:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crossing">[eq:crossing]</a> for functions other than signed coordinates.
  - Explanation: A first failure of the target bound beyond the induction range produces a parameter where equality holds and the semigroup energy is at most the coordinate rate, so only that crossing needs to be excluded.
- **claim-basic-energy** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-basic-energy)): With the preceding notation, $$\label{eq:basic-energy} \mathcal E(d)\ge\sum_i\mathbb E(D_i g)^2\ge\operatorname{Var}(g).$$
  - Explanation: The semigroup energy dominates the Dirichlet energy of the transformed noised function, hence its variance.
- **claim-boolean-spectral-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-fourier-bounds)): Set $$p=\frac{1-m}{2},\qquad w=\min\left\{2p,\;4\sqrt{\frac32}\,p^{3/2}\right\}.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned} W_1(f)&\le w,\label{eq:first-level-bound}\\ \operatorname{Var}(d)&\le r[(1-r)w+rh^2].\label{eq:variance-bound} \end{aligned}$$ If $d_{\rm o}(x)=(d(x)-d(-x))/2$ is the odd part of $d$, then $$\label{eq:odd-variance-bound} \mathbb Ed_{\rm o}^2\le r[(1-r^2)w+r^2h^2].$$
  - Explanation: Booleanity and Holder's inequality bound the first-level weight, the variance of the noised function and the variance of its odd part.
- **claim-central-band** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-central)): Let $f$ be a nonconstant Boolean function, let $0<\rho<1$, and suppose that $s\ge .84$ and $0\le m\le r^2/2$. If $$\label{cen:crossing} b=h-1+s,\qquad \mathcal E(d)\le K=\frac rs,$$ then $f$ is a signed coordinate.
  - Explanation: In the central bias band a crossing forces the function to be a signed coordinate.
- **claim-calibrated-edge** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cen-edge)): For every coordinate edge, $$\label{cen:edge-bound} D_i d\,D_i\!\left(\frac d{H(d)}\right) \ge (D_i g)^2+\Phi\bigl((D_i g)^2\bigr).$$ The functions $j$ and $\Phi$ are nonnegative, increasing, and convex. Furthermore, $\Phi'(v)/v$ is increasing for $v>0$.
  - Explanation: Each edge cost is bounded below by the squared transformed difference plus a convex increasing correction that preserves the exact cost of a noised coordinate.
- **claim-calibration-constants** (bound, [source](#stmt-cen-constants)): At a crossing in the central band, $$\label{cen:four-constants} \frac{\Phi(M)}M<.0063,\qquad p_*<.019,\qquad \frac{p_*}M<.0532,\qquad \frac\Delta M<.008.$$
  - Explanation: Explicit rational bounds hold for the calibration quantities at a crossing in the central band.
- **claim-comparison-function-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-cen-k-bounds)): For $0\le t\le1$, $$\label{cen:k-estimates} 2-t+\tfrac12(1-t)^2\le k(t)\le2-t+\tfrac34(1-t)^2, \qquad 1\le-k'(t)\le\min\{2,1+\tfrac32(1-t)\}.$$ At $t=1$, the derivative is understood as its left limit.
  - Explanation: Two-sided quadratic bounds and derivative bounds hold for the comparison function on the unit interval.
- **claim-central-pointwise** (bound, [source](#stmt-cen-pointwise)): Under the central-band crossing hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:central">10</a>, for every $0\le u\le1$, $$\label{cen:pointwise-bound} D(u)-2\alpha m\|R\|+.88m^2 \ge C_0(g^2-M)^2+.20(u^2-y^2)^2.$$ The inequality is strict whenever $u\ne y$.
  - Explanation: A pointwise remainder inequality holds, certified by positive Bernstein coefficients, and is strict away from the calibration point.
- **claim-strict-variance-gain** (bound, [source](#stmt-cen-strict-variance)): Inequality <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a> holds. If $m>0$, the stronger bound $$\label{cen:positive-margin} \operatorname{Var}g-M>C_0V_0-\frac\Delta{1+p_*}+.016m^2$$ holds. If $m=0$, <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a> is strict unless $g^2=M$ at every point of the cube.
  - Explanation: The variance gain around the calibration holds, with a positive margin when the mean is positive and strictness unless the squared transformed function is constant.
- **claim-intermediate-band** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-intermediate)): Let $f$ be a nonconstant Boolean function and suppose $$\frac{r^2}{2}\le m\le\frac{37}{50},\qquad \frac{21}{25}\le s<1.$$ If $b=h-1+s$, then $\mathcal E(d)>r/s$. Consequently a crossing as in Lemma <a href="#lem:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:crossing">7</a> cannot occur in this range.
  - Explanation: In the intermediate bias band the semigroup energy exceeds the coordinate rate at a putative crossing, so no crossing occurs there.
- **claim-intermediate-pointwise** (bound, [source](#stmt-mid-pointwise)): For every $-1<d<1$, with $V,\Psi$ defined by <a href="#mid:V" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:V">[mid:V]</a> and <a href="#mid:Psi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:Psi">[mid:Psi]</a>, one has $$\label{mid:pointwise-bounds} \begin{array}{ll} \Psi\ge(\frac12-2m^2)V^2,&0\le m\le\frac15,\\[2pt] \Psi\ge-\frac1{100}V,&\frac15\le m\le\frac23,\\[2pt] \Psi\ge-\frac1{50}V,&\frac23\le m\le\frac{37}{50}. \end{array}$$
  - Explanation: A pointwise lower bound on the remaining expression in terms of the tangent deficit holds in three bias ranges.
- **claim-large-bias** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-large-bias)): If $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$ has $0.74\le m<1$ and $0.84\le s\le1$, then $$h-b\le1-s.$$
  - Explanation: For large bias and moderate noise the Hellinger loss bound is proved directly from forward and reverse norm estimates.
- **claim-cube-log-sobolev** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lb-log-sobolev)): For every positive function $a$ on the uniform cube, $$\label{lb:entropy-bound} \operatorname{Ent}(a^2)\le2\mathbb Ea\mathcal Na.$$
  - Explanation: The logarithmic Sobolev inequality holds on the uniform cube with the stated constant.
- **claim-cube-norm-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lb-norm-bounds)): Let $u_0\ge0$ be a real function on the cube, $\tau\ge0$, and $r=e^{-2\tau}$. For every $q>0$, $q\ne1$, put $p_q=1+r(q-1)$. Then $$\label{lb:norm-moments} \mathbb E(T_{e^{-\tau}}u_0)^q \begin{cases} \displaystyle\le(\mathbb Eu_0^{p_q})^{q/p_q},&q>1,\\[2pt] \displaystyle\ge(\mathbb Eu_0^{p_q})^{q/p_q},&0<q<1. \end{cases}$$
  - Explanation: Forward and reverse moment inequalities for the noise semigroup follow from the logarithmic Sobolev inequality.
- **claim-courtade-kumar** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-ck)): Let $X$ be uniform on $\{-1,1\}^n$, let $f$ be Boolean, and let $Y_i=X_iZ_i$, where the $Z_i$ are independent of $X$ and each other, with $\Pr(Z_i=-1)=\varepsilon\in[0,1/2]$. Then $$I(f(X);Y)\le1-h_2(\varepsilon).$$ Signed coordinates attain equality.
  - Explanation: The Hellinger inequality implies the Courtade-Kumar mutual information bound for Boolean functions of a uniform input through a symmetric channel, with equality for signed coordinates.

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-mid-pointwise",
        "exact_quote": "For every $-1<d<1$, with $V,\\Psi$ defined by <a href=\"#mid:V\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"mid:V\">[mid:V]</a> and <a href=\"#mid:Psi\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"mid:Psi\">[mid:Psi]</a>, one has $$\\label{mid:pointwise-bounds}\n \\begin{array}{ll}\n \\Psi\\ge(\\frac12-2m^2)V^2,&0\\le m\\le\\frac15,\\\\[2pt]\n \\Psi\\ge-\\frac1{100}V,&\\frac15\\le m\\le\\frac23,\\\\[2pt]\n \\Psi\\ge-\\frac1{50}V,&\\frac23\\le m\\le\\frac{37}{50}.\n \\end{array}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-119.2"

          "source_id": "PM-119.2"

      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every $-1<d<1$, with $V,\\Psi$ defined by <a href=\"#mid:V\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"mid:V\">[mid:V]</a> and <a href=\"#mid:Psi\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"mid:Psi\">[mid:Psi]</a>, one has $$\\label{mid:pointwise-bounds}\n \\begin{array}{ll}\n \\Psi\\ge(\\frac12-2m^2)V^2,&0\\le m\\le\\frac15,\\\\[2pt]\n \\Psi\\ge-\\frac1{100}V,&\\frac15\\le m\\le\\frac23,\\\\[2pt]\n \\Psi\\ge-\\frac1{50}V,&\\frac23\\le m\\le\\frac{37}{50}.\n \\end{array}$$",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Hellinger profile** ([source](#sec:introduction)): We write $H(t)=\sqrt{1-t^2}$ for $-1\le t\le1$.
- **Increasing function** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A function is *increasing* if it is nondecreasing in each coordinate.
- **Coordinate projections and number operator** ([source](#eq:dirichlet)): Let $\mathbb E_i$ average only coordinate $i$, and define $$D_i=I-\mathbb E_i,\qquad \mathcal N=\sum_{i=1}^n D_i.$$
- **Binary entropy** ([source](#pm-h-28)): For $0\le p\le1$, write $$h_2(p)=-p\log_2p-(1-p)\log_2(1-p),$$ with $0\log_2 0=0$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-compression** [claim](#stmt-ind-lem-compression) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-lem-compression): Coordinatewise sorting of sections with concavity of the pair profile -> increasing function of equal mean with smaller expected profile
- **step-small-coordinate** [claim](#stmt-ind-lem-coordinate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-lem-coordinate): Average over four active coordinates and a fractional rearrangement bound -> coordinate with small first-level coefficient
- **step-scalar-certificates** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar) / [proof](#app:scalar): Interval and Bernstein certificates for pair, profile and base cases -> four scalar assertions
- **step-asymmetric-induction** [claim](#stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric): Cauchy-Schwarz on the pair inequality and monotone root substitution -> asymmetric estimate in all dimensions
- **step-induction-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-induction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric-2): Compress, apply the estimate to the function and its dual, average with the reflection identity -> Hellinger bound in the strong-noise range
- **step-crossing** [claim](#stmt-lem-crossing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-crossing): Left endpoint of a negativity component and the energy derivative identity -> crossing with bounded energy
- **step-basic-energy** [claim](#stmt-lem-basic-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-basic-energy): Edgewise Cauchy-Schwarz after the change of variable -> energy bounded below by variance of g
- **step-spectral-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lem-fourier-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-fourier-bounds): Holder inequality on one-sided linear forms and Parseval -> weight, variance and odd-variance bounds
- **step-calibrated-edge** [claim](#stmt-cen-edge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-edge): Hyperbolic cosine average minimized at a centered interval -> calibrated edge bound with convex correction
- **step-calibration-constants** [claim](#stmt-cen-constants) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-constants): Binomial series lower bound and exact rational comparisons -> four numerical calibration bounds
- **step-comparison-function** [claim](#stmt-cen-k-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-k-bounds): Differential residual signs and backward comparison -> two-sided bounds on the function and its derivative
- **step-central-pointwise** [claim](#stmt-cen-pointwise) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-pointwise): Integral identity and Bernstein sign certificates on two rectangles -> pointwise remainder bound
- **step-strict-variance** [claim](#stmt-cen-strict-variance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-strict-variance): Average the pointwise bound and compare the calibration loss with the mean -> variance gain with margin
- **step-central-band** [claim](#stmt-prop-central) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cen-strict-variance-2): Absorb the first-level spread, then Parseval in the equality case -> crossing forces a signed coordinate
- **step-intermediate-pointwise** [claim](#stmt-mid-pointwise) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mid-pointwise-2): Angular coordinates, positive series expansion and rational polynomial certificates -> three pointwise lower bounds
- **step-intermediate-band** [claim](#stmt-prop-intermediate) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mid-pointwise): Parity energy reduction and bias margins in three ranges -> no crossing in the intermediate band
- **step-log-sobolev** [claim](#stmt-lb-log-sobolev) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lb-log-sobolev): One-bit inequality and entropy tensorization -> logarithmic Sobolev inequality
- **step-norm-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lb-norm-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lb-norm-bounds): Gross semigroup method along a varying exponent path -> forward and reverse moment bounds
- **step-large-bias** [claim](#stmt-prop-large-bias) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lb-norm-bounds-2): Binomial series of moments, moment bounds and exact rational evaluation on four noise intervals -> direct Hellinger bound for large bias
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lb-norm-bounds-3): Case split by noise parameter and bias, with crossing exclusion -> Hellinger conjecture
- **step-courtade-kumar** [claim](#stmt-cor-ck) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-ck): Convex increasing entropy profile, Jensen and fixed-length increments -> mutual information bound

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-binomial-coefficient-mass**: The coefficients of the binomial series for one minus the square root of one minus z are generated by the exact rational recursion starting at one half, and the mass left after the first ten coefficients is exactly 46189/262144.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-nonuniform-input**: Reusing the Hellinger loss bound for inputs drawn from a non-uniform or correlated prior. -> The sharp bound is proved only for the uniform cube measure, so the loss can exceed the stated value.
- **anti-soft-classifier**: Applying the bound to real-valued or soft decision functions. -> The proof uses that the output is a sign, so soft outputs are not covered.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The large-bias proof relies on a finite exact rational computation of the binomial series coefficients and their remaining mass; it is reproduced as an exact-fraction program. The main inequality itself is not finite and is not tested.

```python
# INPUTS: one non-negative integer N on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: N is an integer with 0 <= N; coefficients follow the recursion with first value one half.
# OUTPUTS: one line p/q, the exact reduced fraction equal to one minus the sum of the first N coefficients.
# TIME: O(N) exact rational operations.
# SPACE: O(1) rationals.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Fraction arithmetic, no floating point.
# FAILURE: malformed or missing input raises an exception and a non-zero exit.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
n = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])
c = Fraction(1, 2)
s = Fraction(0)
for k in range(1, n + 1):
    s += c
    c = c * Fraction(2 * k - 1, 2 * (k + 1))
r = 1 - s
print(f"{r.numerator}/{r.denominator}")
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves a mathematical inequality for Boolean functions under product noise. It describes no software artefact or interface. Its only computational content is a set of fixed scalar certificates, which are verification scripts rather than a reusable program. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No simulation, game mechanic or stochastic agent model appears. The noise operator is an analytic tool for proving an inequality, not a simulation scheme. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper supplies no standalone tool. Its exact-arithmetic certificate conventions serve only this proof, so they are not a general-purpose proof-checking utility. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main results are existential inequalities over all Boolean functions. They give no algorithm with input, output and cost. The finite certificates check fixed scalar inequalities independent of the dimension n, not a procedure that solves a problem on inputs. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is defined or analysed. The paper has only Fourier expansions and subdivision trees used as proof devices. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no hardware design or throughput consideration. Its mention of wide integer intermediates concerns only the verification scripts. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No cryptographic primitive, adversary or security notion is considered. The setting is a noisy channel with a single Boolean summary, studied for information content rather than secrecy. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem, hypothesis class or generalisation statement appears. Boolean functions are analysed in the worst case over all functions, not learned from data. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result identifies signed coordinates as extremal for a loss, which is an extremal statement and not an optimisation procedure. No objective is solved computationally and no constraints are modelled. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Distributed sensors are only a motivating reading of the information bound. The paper states no protocol, fault model or communication complexity result, so the link stays interpretive. |
| other (information theory and Boolean function analysis) | True | strong | inspirational | inferred | The theorem bounds the Hellinger affinity loss of a noisy Boolean function and implies the Courtade-Kumar mutual information bound. This is a mathematical contract on the hypercube with uniform input measure. It is not a computational construction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-uniform-input-measure** (hypothesis): The main inequality is proved only for the uniform probability measure on the cube, both for the input and for the expectations in the bound. Blocked use: ['Quoting the sharp loss bound for non-uniform or correlated input priors', 'Using the bound for sources whose bits are biased or dependent']
- **limit-boolean-valued-output** (hypothesis): The theorem covers sign-valued functions only. Soft or real-valued summaries are outside the stated hypothesis. Blocked use: ['Applying the loss bound to real-valued or soft voting functions', 'Reading the bound as a guarantee for probabilistic classifiers']
- **limit-induction-noise-range** (hypothesis): The dimension-induction theorem is stated only when the noise parameter s is at most .84. Beyond that range the argument is replaced by the continuation criterion and the three bias bands. Blocked use: ['Invoking the induction theorem for s above .84', 'Assuming the asymmetric profile estimate holds in the weak-noise regime']
- **limit-central-band-crossing** (hypothesis): The central-band result applies only to a nonconstant function with s at least .84 and small nonnegative mean, and only under the crossing conditions. Its conclusion is that the function is a signed coordinate. Blocked use: ['Using the central-band conclusion for means above the band', 'Using it for functions that do not satisfy the crossing conditions']
- **limit-intermediate-band-range** (hypothesis): The intermediate-bias exclusion of a crossing is proved only for means between the lower band edge and 37/50, with s from 21/25 up to one. Blocked use: ['Extending the no-crossing statement to means above 37/50 by this argument', 'Using it for s below 21/25']
- **limit-large-bias-range** (hypothesis): The direct norm-estimate argument is proved only for means from 0.74 up to but excluding one, and s from 0.84 up to one. The endpoint cases are handled separately. Blocked use: ['Applying the direct large-bias bound at smaller means', 'Applying it for s below 0.84']
- **limit-computer-assisted-certificates** (formal_scope): The proof relies on fixed scalar inequalities certified by exact finite computation. The paper does not claim a machine-checked formal proof of these certificates. Blocked use: ['Describing the whole theorem as formally verified', 'Treating the certificate scripts as general positivity checkers']
- **limit-symmetric-noise-channel** (hypothesis): The information corollary assumes independent symmetric sign flips with crossover at most one half, applied to a uniform input. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to asymmetric channels or channels with memory', 'Using it for crossover probabilities above one half']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the Hellinger conjecture for Boolean functions on the uniform discrete cube, with arbitrary output bias. For a Boolean function of mean <i>m</i> and every $`\rho\in[-1,1]`$, the loss $`\sqrt{1-m^2}-\mathbb E\sqrt{1-(T_\rho f)^2}`$ is at most $`1-\sqrt{1-\rho^2}`$, with equality for signed coordinates. The proof combines asymmetric dimension induction, a calibrated noise-semigroup energy estimate, and finite exact arithmetic certificates. The Hellinger inequality also yields the Courtade–Kumar information bound.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The Hellinger conjecture asks whether a single coordinate maximizes a particular loss of Hellinger affinity under product noise, among all Boolean functions on a uniform cube. Its formulation retains the mean of the function, so biased functions are part of the question. Anantharam, Bogdanov, Chakrabarti, Jayram, and Nair formulated it in their 2017 manuscript and proved that it implies the Courtade–Kumar conjecture on the most informative Boolean function <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[ABCJN](#ref-ABCJN), [CK14](#ref-CK14)]. This connection makes a sharp inequality for the square-root profile significant beyond that profile itself.

Let $X$ be uniform on $\{-1,1\}^n$. For $-1\le\rho\le1$, let $Y_i=X_i Z_i$, where the independent signs $Z_i$, also independent of $X$, satisfy $\Pr(Z_i=1)=(1+\rho)/2$. The noise operator is $$T_\rho u(x)=\mathbb E[u(Y)\mid X=x].$$ We write $H(t)=\sqrt{1-t^2}$ for $-1\le t\le1$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Hellinger conjecture). *For every $n\ge1$, every Boolean function $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$, and every $\rho\in[-1,1]$, writing $m=\mathbb Ef$, one has $$\label{eq:main}
H(m)-\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)\le1-H(\rho).$$ Both expectations are with respect to the uniform probability measure.*

</div>

Thus the conjecture is resolved positively in its full, unbalanced form. Signed coordinate functions $f(x)=\pm x_i$, also called dictators, attain equality for every $\rho$. The implication proved in <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[ABCJN](#ref-ABCJN), Proposition 1] also shows that signed coordinates maximize the mutual information between $f(X)$ and $Y$, which is the Courtade–Kumar conclusion. We state and prove this implication in Corollary <a href="#cor:ck" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:ck">21</a>, keeping its binary-entropy normalization explicit. Throughout the paper the input measure is uniform; arbitrary output bias means that no restriction is imposed on $m$.

To identify its Hellinger meaning, suppose $f$ is nonconstant and let $P_\pm$ be the conditional law of $Y$ given $f(X)=\pm1$. Bayes’ formula and symmetry of the noise kernel give $P_\pm(y)=2^{-n}(1\pm T_\rho f(y))/(1\pm m)$. Their Hellinger affinity is $$A(P_+,P_-):=\sum_y\sqrt{P_+(y)P_-(y)}
 =\frac{\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)}{H(m)}.$$ Thus the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> is the weighted affinity loss $H(m)(1-A(P_+,P_-))$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### Earlier results and methods.

The original information problem of Courtade and Kumar <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CK14](#ref-CK14)] asks how much information a single Boolean summary retains about a noisy input. The square-root formulation of Anantharam et al. <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[ABCJN](#ref-ABCJN)] replaces binary entropy by Hellinger affinity while preserving an implication to that information bound. It also supplies a compression principle for arbitrary convex profiles, whose concave form we use in Section <a href="#sec:induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:induction">3</a>.

Chen and Nair <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[ChenNair24](#ref-ChenNair24), Theorem 2, Equation (6), and Remark 5] proved a mean-dependent lower bound using the Gaussian isoperimetric profile. Let $\phi_{\rm G}$ and $\Phi_{\rm G}$ be the standard Gaussian density and distribution function, and put $I_{\rm G}(p)=\phi_{\rm G}(\Phi_{\rm G}^{-1}(p))$ for $0<p<1$, with $I_{\rm G}(0)=I_{\rm G}(1)=0$. Writing $s=\sqrt{1-\rho^2}$, their estimate is $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)\ge 2s\,I_{\rm G}\!\left(\frac{1+m}{2}\right).$$ This bound holds for every Boolean mean. It implies the Hellinger inequality wherever its right side reaches $H(m)-1+s$, but at $m=0$ it gives only $\sqrt{2/\pi}\,s$, below the sharp value $s$ for $s>0$. Their proof in Section III.B splits the cube into two sections and closes a recursion for a profile of their means. It connects this induction to Bobkov’s local two-point inequality and its tensorization on the discrete cube <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Bobkov97](#ref-Bobkov97), Proposition 1 and Lemma 1]. The induction below uses this local-to-product strategy with an asymmetric profile and a sheared two-point comparison; its scalar inequalities are proved here.

Durcik, Ivanisvili, Roos, and Xie <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DIRX26](#ref-DIRX26), Theorem 1.1 and Section 5] proved the sharp square-root sensitivity inequality for balanced Boolean functions, confirming the low-noise consequence predicted by the Hellinger conjecture.

Recent preprints of Chen et al. <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026CK](#ref-ChenEtAl2026CK)], Ky and Tran <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KyTran2026CK](#ref-KyTran2026CK)], and Mahdavifar and Beirami <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[MahdavifarBeirami2026CK](#ref-MahdavifarBeirami2026CK)] announce proofs of the ordinary Courtade–Kumar inequality. The Hellinger inequality studied here is a distinct functional statement: the implication in <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ABCJN](#ref-ABCJN)] runs from the Hellinger estimate to the ordinary Boolean information bound.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### Proof strategy.

A differential approach to the Hellinger conjecture was developed by Chen, Gohari, and Nair in 2025 . They express the noise-semigroup derivative of $\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)$ as a sum of edge costs and bound that derivative through scalar profiles. We use the same derivative identity, in the normalization specified in Section <a href="#sec:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuation">4</a>. The calibrated edge comparison and the spectral estimates needed for our continuation argument are proved below.

There are two complementary parts to the proof. For $s\le .84$, we compress to increasing Boolean functions and induct in dimension. For each fixed $s$ we choose a concave function $G:[0,1]\to[0,\infty)$ and a nonnegative polynomial $B$ on $[-1,1]$, and prove the stronger bound $$\mathbb EG\bigl((1+T_\rho f)/2\bigr)\ge sB(m).$$ The identity $G(p)+G(1-p)=2H(2p-1)$ recovers the Hellinger profile when this bound is averaged with the bound for the increasing function $-f(-x)$. The polynomial $B$ is chosen so that the reflected lower bound is at least $H(m)-1+s$. The induction step controls the gain from mixing two sections by their probability difference after subtracting a multiple of their difference in $G$. Cauchy–Schwarz then gives an inequality involving only the section averages and their mean gap. Its monotone quadratic-root bound allows substitution of the induction hypotheses. A coordinate with small positive correlation with the output confines the comparison to a fixed scalar domain.

For $s\ge .84$, we continue from the established range. At a first failure of the target bound, its equality case would have semigroup energy no greater than the corresponding coordinate energy. The change of variable $F(0)=0$, $F'=H^{-3/2}$ bounds the energy below by the Dirichlet energy of $F(T_\rho f)$. Near zero bias, a sharper edge bound uses a symmetric comparison pair $\pm x_0$ with $H(x_0)=\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)$. This retains the exact cost of a noised coordinate. The resulting energy balance separates a variance gain from the possible spread of the first Fourier level; controlling that spread excludes a crossing unless $f$ is a signed coordinate. For intermediate biases, even–odd Fourier decomposition reduces the energy estimate to pointwise bounds with explicit bias margins. Large biases instead admit a direct proof from forward and reverse cube norm estimates. Figure <a href="#fig:proof-regions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-regions">1</a> displays how their parameter ranges fit together.

The proof is computer-assisted only through fixed scalar inequalities, independent of $n$. Every decimal appearing as an inequality constant denotes its exact rational value. We give the transformations, domains, arithmetic recursions, and positive bounds that certify these inequalities; the supplementary sources reproduce the finite calculations.

<figure id="fig:proof-regions">

<figcaption>The proof covers all bias and noise parameters. The initial induction range supplies the starting point for continuation; the three lower boxes cover every possible nonnegative bias at a proposed crossing. The large-bias estimate proves the target directly. Endpoints and negative means are handled by continuity and symmetry.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes the cube conventions. Section <a href="#sec:induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:induction">3</a> proves the dimension-induction range. Section <a href="#sec:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuation">4</a> reduces the rest to excluding an energy crossing. Sections <a href="#sec:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:central">5</a>, <a href="#sec:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:intermediate">6</a>, and <a href="#sec:large-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:large-bias">7</a> treat the three bias regimes and complete the proof. Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">9</a> records the exact-arithmetic conventions; Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a> proves the scalar estimates needed for the induction.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Cube conventions and elementary reductions

All averages and inner products below use the uniform measure on the relevant cube. For a real function $u$ on $\{-1,1\}^n$, write $$\chi_S(x)=\prod_{i\in S}x_i,\qquad
\widehat u(S)=\mathbb E[u\chi_S],\qquad
u=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}\widehat u(S)\chi_S.$$ The functions $\chi_S$ form an orthonormal basis: independence makes $\mathbb E\chi_S\chi_T$ equal to $1$ if $S=T$ and to $0$ otherwise. Consequently, $$\operatorname{Var}(u)=\sum_{S\ne\varnothing}\widehat u(S)^2.$$ For later use set $W_k(u)=\sum_{|S|=k}\widehat u(S)^2$.

<a id="eq:dirichlet"></a>
Let $\mathbb E_i$ average only coordinate $i$, and define $$D_i=I-\mathbb E_i,\qquad \mathcal N=\sum_{i=1}^n D_i.$$ Thus $D_i u(x)=(u(x)-u(x^{(i)}))/2$, where $x^{(i)}$ reverses coordinate $i$. The projections $D_i$ are self-adjoint and satisfy $$\label{eq:dirichlet}
\mathbb E[u\mathcal N v]
=\sum_i\mathbb E[D_i u\,D_i v]
=\sum_S |S|\,\widehat u(S)\widehat v(S).$$ Independence in the noise definition gives $T_\rho\chi_S=\rho^{|S|}\chi_S$. Hence, for $\tau\ge0$, $T_{e^{-\tau}}=e^{-\tau\mathcal N}$. These elementary facts are also standard in the analysis of Boolean functions <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ODonnell14](#ref-ODonnell14)]; their normalizations are specified here to fix every factor of two.

We first remove the endpoints and symmetries. The identity $$T_{-\rho}f(x)=T_\rho f(-x)$$ shows that it suffices to consider $\rho\ge0$. At $\rho=0$, the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> is zero. At $\rho=1$, it equals $H(m)\le1$. For a constant $f$, it is again zero. For $0<\rho<1$ and nonconstant $f$, every input has positive noise probability, so $$-1<T_\rho f(x)<1\qquad\text{for every }x.$$ All differentiations involving $H$ below are therefore legitimate. Finally, replacing $f$ by $-f$ preserves both sides of Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> and reverses $m$.

<a id="sec:induction"></a>
### Dimension induction

We first prove the desired inequality in the range reached by dimension induction. Every decimal in this section denotes an exact rational number.

<a id="stmt-thm-induction"></a>
<div id="thm:induction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *Let $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$, let $m=\mathbb Ef$, and let $0<\rho<1$. If $s=\sqrt{1-\rho^2}\le .84$, then $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)\ge H(m)-1+s.$$*

</div>

We strengthen the desired bound to an inequality for a nonsymmetric function of the noisy output. Averaging this inequality with its reflected version will recover $H$. The induction uses an asymmetric function $G$ and a polynomial profile $B$. The scalar inequalities that support it are stated in Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar">5</a> and proved by the finite arithmetic certificates in Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a>. We first establish the two reductions that explain their domains.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Compression and a small coordinate

The following sorting argument is the concave-profile form of the compression principle of Anantharam, Bogdanov, Chakrabarti, Jayram, and Nair <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ABCJN](#ref-ABCJN), Proposition 2]. We include the short proof to specify its direction for $H$. We order the cube coordinatewise using $-1<1$. A function is *increasing* if it is nondecreasing in each coordinate.

<a id="stmt-ind-lem-compression"></a>
<div id="ind:lem:compression" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Compression). *For every Boolean function $f$ there is an increasing Boolean function $\widetilde f$ with the same mean such that $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho\widetilde f)\le \mathbb EH(T_\rho f).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-lem-compression"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-lem-compression</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a coordinate, and write $f_-,f_+$ for its two sections. Replace them pointwise by $\min(f_-,f_+),\max(f_-,f_+)$, respectively. Let $T$ denote noise in the remaining coordinates and put $$a=T\bigl((f_-+f_+)/2\bigr),\qquad
 c=T\bigl((f_+-f_-)/2\bigr).$$ Sorting preserves $a$ and replaces $c$ by $$c'=T\bigl(|f_+-f_-|/2\bigr)\ge |c|,$$ since $T$ has a nonnegative kernel. The two fully noised values are $a\pm\rho c$ before sorting and $a\pm\rho c'$ afterwards. For fixed $a$, the function $u\mapsto H(a+u)+H(a-u)$ is even and concave on its domain, and hence nonincreasing for $u\ge0$. Thus sorting weakly decreases the pair average of $H$. It preserves the mean.

Perform this operation once in each coordinate. If the function is already increasing in a different coordinate, both its sections are increasing in that coordinate; their pointwise minimum and maximum remain increasing. The resulting function is therefore increasing in every coordinate. ◻

</details>

Call a coordinate *active* if the function depends on it. Inactive coordinates may be omitted from all expectations below. Define <a id="ind:eq:b"></a>$$\label{ind:eq:b}
 b(m)=\min\left\{\frac38,\frac9{16}-\frac{|m|}{2},1-|m|\right\},
 \qquad -1\le m\le1.$$

<a id="stmt-ind-lem-coordinate"></a>
<div id="ind:lem:coordinate" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Choice of a coordinate). *If an increasing Boolean function of mean $m$ has at least four active coordinates, one of them has first-level coefficient $$0<x=\mathbb E[f(X)X_i]\le b(m).$$ The two sections in this coordinate have means $m-x$ and $m+x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-lem-coordinate"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-lem-coordinate</summary>

*Proof.* For an increasing Boolean function, the coefficient of coordinate $i$ is $$x_i=\mathbb E\bigl[(f_+-f_-)/2\bigr].$$ The integrand takes values in $\{0,1\}$, so $x_i>0$ precisely when the coordinate is active. Choose a coordinate with the least active coefficient $x$. For any four distinct active coordinates, $$x\le\frac{x_{i_1}+x_{i_2}+x_{i_3}+x_{i_4}}4
   =\mathbb E[f(X)Z],\qquad
 Z=\frac{X_{i_1}+X_{i_2}+X_{i_3}+X_{i_4}}4.$$ The distribution of $Z$, in decreasing order, is $$\begin{array}{c|rrrrr}
z&1&1/2&0&-1/2&-1\\ \hline
\Pr(Z=z)&1/16&4/16&6/16&4/16&1/16.
\end{array}$$ At fixed mean $m$, the largest possible $\mathbb E[fZ]$ is obtained by placing the positive values of $f$ at the largest values of $Z$, allowing a fraction of the threshold level. Indeed, transfer of positive mass from a smaller value of $Z$ to a larger one can only increase the objective; allowing fractional values is a relaxation and therefore gives an upper bound for Boolean $f$. Since $\mathbb EZ=0$ and the law of $Z$ is symmetric, this maximum is twice the integral over its top fraction $q=(1-|m|)/2$. For $q\in[0,1/2]$ that quantity is $$\begin{cases}
 2q,&0\le q\le1/16,\\
 q+1/16,&1/16\le q\le5/16,\\
 3/8,&5/16\le q\le1/2.
 \end{cases}$$ These are exactly the three pieces of $b(m)$. Finally, the section means have average $m$ and half-difference $x$, proving the last claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The scalar interface

For the fixed value of $s$, use one row of Table <a href="#ind:tab:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:tab:parameters">1</a> whose interval contains $s$. The profile polynomials use a finer subdivision of these same intervals, specified in Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a>. At a shared endpoint, choose the parameters and the profile consistently from one adjoining interval. Put $\delta=(1-\rho)/2$, so that $4\delta(1-\delta)=s^2$; the auxiliary bounds in the table satisfy $r_0^2\le\delta\le r_1^2$.

<div id="ind:tab:parameters">

| $i$ | $s_{\rm lo}$ | $s_{\rm hi}$ | $r_0$ | $r_1$ | $L$ |    $U$ | $V$ | $\alpha$ | $\Lambda$ | $\mu$ |
|----:|-------------:|-------------:|------:|------:|----:|-------:|----:|---------:|----------:|------:|
|   0 |            0 |           70 |     0 |    36 | 940 | $-440$ | 300 |      960 |      1000 |   956 |
|   1 |           70 |          160 |    35 |    81 | 940 | $-440$ | 300 |      930 |      1050 |   953 |
|   2 |          160 |          300 |    80 |   152 | 886 | $-398$ | 250 |      860 |      1150 |   985 |
|   3 |          300 |          490 |   151 |   254 | 842 | $-335$ | 180 |      770 |      1150 |  1024 |
|   4 |          490 |          630 |   253 |   335 | 842 | $-335$ | 180 |      690 |      1150 |  1058 |
|   5 |          630 |          750 |   334 |   412 | 842 | $-335$ | 180 |      632 |      1160 |  1087 |
|   6 |          750 |          840 |   411 |   479 | 842 | $-335$ | 180 |      583 |      1160 |  1111 |

Induction parameters. Every displayed entry except $i$ is $1000$ times the corresponding parameter.

</div>

<a id="ind:eq:G"></a>
<a id="ind:eq:G-reflection"></a>
Define $$\label{ind:eq:G}
 \begin{split}
 \lambda(h)&=L+Uh+Vh^2,\\
 G(p)&=H(2p-1)\left(1+
 \frac{2p-1}{1+H(2p-1)\lambda(H(2p-1))}\right),\qquad 0\le p\le1.
 \end{split}$$ All rows have $U<0$, $V\ge0$, and $L+U\ge .488$. Thus $\lambda(h)\ge L+U>0$ for $0\le h\le1$, and $G$ is nonnegative, with $G(0)=G(1)=0$. Its defining asymmetry cancels under reflection: $$\label{ind:eq:G-reflection}
 G(p)+G(1-p)=2H(2p-1).$$

The profiles in the next lemma are explicit polynomials of the form <a id="ind:eq:profile-def"></a>$$\label{ind:eq:profile-def}
 B(m)=(1-m^2)P(m),\qquad
 P(m)=1+10^{-8}\sum_{j\ge1}c_j\bigl(T_j(m)-T_j(0)\bigr),$$ where only finitely many $c_j$ are nonzero, and $T_0(u)=1$, $T_1(u)=u$, $T_{j+1}(u)=2uT_j(u)-T_{j-1}(u)$. The integer coefficients and their arithmetic verification are given in Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a>.

With these functions, the estimate we will propagate is $$\mathbb EG\bigl((1+T_\rho f)/2\bigr)\ge sB(m)
 \qquad(m=\mathbb Ef)$$ for every increasing Boolean function $f$. Its dual $f^*(x)=-f(-x)$ is increasing and has mean $-m$. Noise commutes with input reversal, so averaging the proposed estimates for $f$ and $f^*$ and using Equation <a href="#ind:eq:G-reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:G-reflection">[ind:eq:G-reflection]</a> would give $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)\ge\frac{s}{2}\bigl(B(m)+B(-m)\bigr).$$ Thus we need a profile whose reflected average reaches $H(m)-1+s$, and an estimate for $G$ that passes from two sections to their parent. The next lemma supplies the two-point gap used in this passage, the reflected comparison, the condition that closes induction on the small-coordinate domain, and the initial cases.

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Scalar inequalities). *For every $0<s\le .84$, choose the parameters and the polynomial $B$ as above. Then $B\ge0$ on $[-1,1]$, $B(0)=1$, $B(\pm1)=0$, and the following four assertions hold.*

<a id="ind:eq:pair"></a>
1.  *For $0\le p_0\le p_1\le1$, write $z=p_1-p_0$ and $p_u=p_0+uz$, and set $$D=\frac{G(p_\delta)+G(p_{1-\delta})-G(p_0)-G(p_1)}2,
     \qquad S=\alpha G(p_1)+(1-\alpha)G(p_0).$$ Then $D\ge0$ and $$\label{ind:eq:pair}
     D(S+\Lambda D)\ge
     \mu s^2\left(z-\frac{G(p_1)-G(p_0)}{4L}\right)^2.$$*

<a id="ind:eq:reflection-profile"></a>
2.  *For every $m\in[-1,1]$, $$\label{ind:eq:reflection-profile}
     \frac{s}{2}\bigl(B(m)+B(-m)\bigr)\ge H(m)-1+s.$$*

<a id="ind:eq:profile"></a>
3.  *For $m\in[-1,1]$ and $0<x\le b(m)$, put $$B_\pm=B(m\pm x),\qquad
     C=\frac{B_+-B_-}{2x},\qquad
     A=\frac{2B(m)-B_+-B_-}{2x^2}.$$ Then $$\label{ind:eq:profile}
     A\bigl[B(m)+(2\alpha-1)xC+(\Lambda-1)x^2A\bigr]
     \le \mu\left(1-\frac{s}{2L}C\right)^2.$$*

<a id="ind:eq:base"></a>
4.  *Every increasing Boolean function depending on at most three coordinates satisfies $$\label{ind:eq:base}
     \mathbb EG\bigl((1+T_\rho f)/2\bigr)\ge sB(\mathbb Ef).$$*

</div>

The four assertions of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar">5</a>, including the entire three-coordinate base case, are proved in Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a>. We now show how the pair bound and profile comparison propagate the asymmetric estimate from three active coordinates to arbitrary dimension.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Averaging and dimension induction

<a id="stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric"></a>
<div id="ind:prop:asymmetric" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6**. *For the fixed $s$, parameters, and profile above, every increasing Boolean function satisfies <a id="ind:eq:all-dimensions"></a>$$\label{ind:eq:all-dimensions}
 \mathbb EG\bigl((1+T_\rho f)/2\bigr)\ge sB(\mathbb Ef).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric</summary>

*Proof.* Induct on the number of active coordinates. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar">5</a>(4) provides the cases with at most three. Otherwise choose the coordinate in Lemma <a href="#ind:lem:coordinate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:lem:coordinate">4</a>, and denote its increasing sections by $f_-$ and $f_+$, with means $m-x$ and $m+x$. On the smaller cube let $$p_0=\frac{1+T_\rho f_-}{2},\qquad
 p_1=\frac{1+T_\rho f_+}{2}.$$ In these expressions, $T_\rho$ acts only on the remaining coordinates. Positivity of its kernel gives $p_0\le p_1$, and $\mathbb E(p_1-p_0)=x$. At the two values of the chosen coordinate the parent noise probabilities are $p_\delta$ and $p_{1-\delta}$.

<a id="ind:eq:averaged-pair"></a>
<a id="ind:eq:R"></a>
Apply Equation <a href="#ind:eq:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:pair">[ind:eq:pair]</a> pointwise, allowing $p_0=p_1$, in which case both $D$ and the expression on the right vanish. Set $$y=\frac{\mathbb EG(p_1)}s,\qquad t=\frac{\mathbb EG(p_0)}s,\qquad
 e=\frac{\mathbb ED}s,\qquad k=\frac{s}{2L},\qquad
 Q=\alpha y+(1-\alpha)t.$$ All of $y,t,e,Q$ are nonnegative. To average the pair inequality, write $w=p_1-p_0-(G(p_1)-G(p_0))/(4L)$. Cauchy–Schwarz yields $$\begin{split}
 (\mathbb ED)\,\mathbb E(S+\Lambda D)
 &\ge\left(\mathbb E\sqrt{D(S+\Lambda D)}\right)^2\\
 &\ge\mu s^2(\mathbb E|w|)^2
 \ge\mu s^2(\mathbb Ew)^2.
 \end{split}$$ No fixed sign of $w$ is required. Since $\mathbb Ew=x-k(y-t)/2$, division by $s^2$ gives $$\label{ind:eq:averaged-pair}
 e(Q+\Lambda e)\ge\mu\left[x-\frac{k}{2}(y-t)\right]^2.$$ The parent expectation divided by $s$ equals $(y+t)/2+e$. Solving Equation <a href="#ind:eq:averaged-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:averaged-pair">[ind:eq:averaged-pair]</a> for the nonnegative variable $e$ therefore gives the lower bound $$\label{ind:eq:R}
 R_x(y,t)=\frac{y+t}{2}+
 \frac{\sqrt{Q^2+4\Lambda\mu v^2}-Q}{2\Lambda},
 \qquad v=x-\frac{k}{2}(y-t).$$

<a id="ind:eq:root-derivative"></a>
We next justify substitution of the induction bounds into this root. Put $\Delta=\sqrt{Q^2+4\Lambda\mu v^2}$. For $y,t>0$, $$\begin{split}
 \partial_yR_x
 &=\frac12-\frac{\alpha}{2\Lambda}
    +\frac{\alpha Q-2\Lambda\mu kv}{2\Lambda\Delta},\\
 \partial_tR_x
 &=\frac12-\frac{1-\alpha}{2\Lambda}
    +\frac{(1-\alpha)Q+2\Lambda\mu kv}{2\Lambda\Delta}.
 \end{split}$$ Using $Q\ge0$ and $|v|/\Delta\le1/(2\sqrt{\Lambda\mu})$, each derivative is at least $$\label{ind:eq:root-derivative}
 \frac12\left(1-\frac{\max(\alpha,1-\alpha)}{\Lambda}
              -k\sqrt{\frac\mu\Lambda}\right)>0.$$ For completeness, the strict sign follows by rational arithmetic from Table <a href="#ind:tab:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:tab:parameters">1</a>. If $a=1-\max(\alpha,1-\alpha)/\Lambda$, every row has $a>0$ and $$a^2-\left(\frac{s_{\rm hi}}{2L}\right)^2\frac\mu\Lambda
 \ge\frac{12133}{44180000}>0.$$ Since $s\le s_{\rm hi}$, this proves the sign in Equation <a href="#ind:eq:root-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:root-derivative">[ind:eq:root-derivative]</a>. Continuity extends coordinatewise monotonicity of $R_x$ to the whole nonnegative quadrant.

Each section has fewer active coordinates, so the induction hypothesis implies $y\ge B_+$ and $t\ge B_-$. We have consequently obtained the lower bound $R_x(B_+,B_-)$ for the normalized parent expectation. In the notation of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar">5</a>(3), at these arguments $$\frac{B_++B_-}{2}=B(m)-x^2A,\qquad
 Q=B(m)-x^2A+(2\alpha-1)xC,\qquad
 v=x(1-kC).$$ If $A\le0$, the first of these terms is already at least $B(m)$, and the root correction in Equation <a href="#ind:eq:R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:R">[ind:eq:R]</a> is nonnegative. Suppose $A>0$. The correction is the nonnegative solution $u$ of $u(Q+\Lambda u)=\mu x^2(1-kC)^2$. Because $Q\ge0$ and $\Lambda>0$, the left side is increasing for $u\ge0$. Equation <a href="#ind:eq:profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:profile">[ind:eq:profile]</a>, multiplied by $x^2$, states that $$\begin{split}
 (x^2A)(Q+\Lambda x^2A)
 &=x^2A\bigl[B(m)+(2\alpha-1)xC+(\Lambda-1)x^2A\bigr]\\
 &\le\mu x^2(1-kC)^2.
 \end{split}$$ Thus the correction is at least $x^2A$, and again $R_x(B_+,B_-)\ge B(m)$. This completes the induction. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-prop-asymmetric</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:induction">2</a>.* By Lemma <a href="#ind:lem:compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:lem:compression">3</a>, it suffices to consider increasing $f$. Its dual $f^*(x)=-f(-x)$ is also increasing and has mean $-m$. Noise commutes with input reversal, so $T_\rho f^*(x)=-T_\rho f(-x)$. Apply Proposition <a href="#ind:prop:asymmetric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:prop:asymmetric">6</a> to $f$ and $f^*$, average the two inequalities, and change variables $x\mapsto-x$ in the second expectation. Equation <a href="#ind:eq:G-reflection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:G-reflection">[ind:eq:G-reflection]</a> gives $$\mathbb EH(T_\rho f)
 \ge\frac{s}{2}\bigl(B(m)+B(-m)\bigr)
 \ge H(m)-1+s,$$ where the last inequality is Equation <a href="#ind:eq:reflection-profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:reflection-profile">[ind:eq:reflection-profile]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:continuation"></a>
### A continuation criterion

The notation in this section and the next three is independent of the induction parameters. Fix a nonconstant Boolean $f$, use output-sign symmetry to take $m=\mathbb Ef\ge0$, and put <a id="eq:continuation-notation"></a>$$\label{eq:continuation-notation}
\begin{gathered}
s=\sqrt{1-\rho^2},\qquad r=\rho^2=1-s^2,\qquad d=T_\rho f,\\
h=H(m),\qquad b=\mathbb EH(d),\qquad v=1-s,\qquad K=\frac rs.
\end{gathered}$$ We work with $.84\le s<1$, so $0<r\le .2944$.

<a id="eq:energy-derivative"></a>
Define <a id="eq:energy-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:energy-definition}
\mathcal E(d)=\mathbb E\sum_i D_i d\,D_i\!\left(\frac{d}{H(d)}\right).$$ For the parameter $\tau=-\log\rho$, one has $\partial_\tau d=-\mathcal N d$. Since $H'(z)=-z/H(z)$, Equation <a href="#eq:dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dirichlet">[eq:dirichlet]</a> yields $$\label{eq:energy-derivative}
\frac{db}{d\tau}=\mathcal E(d),
\qquad
\frac{ds}{d\tau}=\frac rs=K.$$

This identity is the Hellinger edge derivative of Chen, Gohari, and Nair <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[HellingerDifferential](#ref-HellingerDifferential), Section III, Lemma 6] in our generator normalization: their parameter has $\rho=e^{-2t}$, so $\tau=2t$ here.

<a id="stmt-lem-crossing"></a>
<div id="lem:crossing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:crossing"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Crossing criterion). *Suppose Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> is known for $s\le .84$. If it fails for some $s>.84$, then for the same function there is a parameter $.84\le s<1$ for which $$\label{eq:crossing}
b=h-1+s,\qquad \mathcal E(d)\le K.$$ It is enough to exclude Equation <a href="#eq:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crossing">[eq:crossing]</a> for functions other than signed coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-crossing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-crossing</summary>

*Proof.* The desired inequality is $b-h+1-s\ge0$. If this expression is negative at some finite semigroup time, take the left endpoint $\tau_0$ of the connected component of strict negativity containing that time, restricted to times after $s=.84$. Continuity gives equality at $\tau_0$. The right difference quotients there are nonpositive, so differentiability and Equation <a href="#eq:energy-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-derivative">[eq:energy-derivative]</a> give the energy inequality in Equation <a href="#eq:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crossing">[eq:crossing]</a>. For $f=\pm x_i$, one has $m=0$, $d=\pm\rho x_i$, and $b=s$, so the desired inequality is an identity for every parameter. ◻

</details>

The following change of variable will be used in the two smaller bias regimes: <a id="eq:F-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:F-definition}
F(x)=\int_0^x(1-z^2)^{-3/4}\,dz,\qquad g=F(d).$$ The function $F$ is odd, increasing, and has finite limits at $\pm1$.

<a id="stmt-lem-basic-energy"></a>
<div id="lem:basic-energy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Basic energy comparison). *With the preceding notation, <a id="eq:basic-energy"></a>$$\label{eq:basic-energy}
\mathcal E(d)\ge\sum_i\mathbb E(D_i g)^2\ge\operatorname{Var}(g).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-basic-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-basic-energy</summary>

*Proof.* For the two values $z_-\le z_+$ of $d$ on an edge, the edge contributions have the respective forms $$\frac14(z_+-z_-)\left(\frac{z_+}{H(z_+)}-\frac{z_-}{H(z_-)}\right),
\qquad
\frac14(F(z_+)-F(z_-))^2.$$ The derivative of $x/H(x)$ is $H(x)^{-3}=(F'(x))^2$, so Cauchy–Schwarz on $[z_-,z_+]$ compares these in the required direction. The case $z_-=z_+$ follows by continuity. Summing over edges proves the first inequality. The second follows from Equation <a href="#eq:dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dirichlet">[eq:dirichlet]</a>, since every nonconstant Fourier degree is at least one. ◻

</details>

The two smaller bias ranges will combine these energy comparisons with upper bounds on the variance of $d$ and its odd part. Booleanity supplies the following bounds.

<a id="stmt-lem-fourier-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:fourier-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:first-level-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:variance-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:odd-variance-bound"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Boolean spectral bounds). *Set $$p=\frac{1-m}{2},\qquad
w=\min\left\{2p,\;4\sqrt{\frac32}\,p^{3/2}\right\}.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned}
W_1(f)&\le w,\label{eq:first-level-bound}\\
\operatorname{Var}(d)&\le r[(1-r)w+rh^2].\label{eq:variance-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ If $d_{\rm o}(x)=(d(x)-d(-x))/2$ is the odd part of $d$, then $$\label{eq:odd-variance-bound}
\mathbb Ed_{\rm o}^2\le r[(1-r^2)w+r^2h^2].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fourier-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fourier-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Write $L_+=\max(L,0)$ and $L_-=\max(-L,0)$. For a homogeneous linear form $L=\sum_i a_i x_i$ with $\mathbb EL^2=1$, symmetry gives $\mathbb EL_+^2=1/2$, while expansion gives $$\mathbb EL^4=3\left(\sum_i a_i^2\right)^2-2\sum_i a_i^4\le3,
\qquad \mathbb EL_+^4\le3/2.$$ Let $A=\{f=-1\}$, of measure $p$. Since $\mathbb EL=0$, $\mathbb EfL=-2\mathbb E[1_A L]$. The absolute value of the last integral is at most the larger of $\mathbb E[1_A L_+]$ and $\mathbb E[1_A L_-]$. Hölder’s inequality therefore gives $$|\mathbb EfL|\le
2\min\left\{\sqrt{p/2},\;(3/2)^{1/4}p^{3/4}\right\}.$$ Maximizing over $L$ and squaring proves Equation <a href="#eq:first-level-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-level-bound">[eq:first-level-bound]</a>. Parseval and Booleanity give $\sum_{k\ge1}W_k(f)=1-m^2=h^2$. Thus $$\operatorname{Var}(d)=\sum_{k\ge1}r^kW_k(f)
\le rW_1(f)+r^2(h^2-W_1(f)),$$ which proves Equation <a href="#eq:variance-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variance-bound">[eq:variance-bound]</a>. The odd part retains only odd degrees. Every retained degree above one is at least three, so $$\mathbb Ed_{\rm o}^2
\le rW_1(f)+r^3(h^2-W_1(f)),$$ proving Equation <a href="#eq:odd-variance-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-variance-bound">[eq:odd-variance-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

At a hypothetical crossing, the fixed mean lies either in the central band $0\le m\le r^2/2$, in the intermediate band $r^2/2\le m\le .74$, or in the large-bias band $.74\le m<1$. The next sections exclude the crossing in the first two bands and prove the target directly in the third.

<a id="sec:central"></a>
### The central band

We retain the notation of Section <a href="#sec:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuation">4</a>. In the band containing the signed coordinates, we sharpen the basic energy comparison to preserve their exact edge cost. The resulting bound will force any function satisfying the crossing conditions to be a signed coordinate.

<a id="stmt-prop-central"></a>
<div id="prop:central" class="proposition">

<a id="cen:crossing"></a>
**Proposition 10**. *Let $f$ be a nonconstant Boolean function, let $0<\rho<1$, and suppose that $s\ge .84$ and $0\le m\le r^2/2$. If $$\label{cen:crossing}
 b=h-1+s,\qquad \mathcal E(d)\le K=\frac rs,$$ then $f$ is a signed coordinate.*

</div>

<a id="cen:domain"></a>
Throughout the proof all terminating decimals denote exact rational numbers. Under <a href="#cen:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:crossing">[cen:crossing]</a>, set <a id="cen:calibration"></a>$$\label{cen:calibration}
 y=\sqrt b,\qquad x_0=\sqrt{1-b^2},\qquad
 \alpha=\frac{F(x_0)}{x_0},\qquad M=F(x_0)^2.$$ The symbol $\alpha$ is local to this section. Write $R_0=.2944$. Since $0<r\le R_0$ and $m\le R_0^2/2$, the exact comparison $(.999)^2<1-R_0^4/4$ gives $$\label{cen:domain}
 \begin{gathered}
 h>.999,\qquad .839<b\le s<1,\qquad y>.91595>.915,\\
 0\le s-b=\frac{m^2}{1+h}<.001,\qquad M\ge x_0^2\ge r>0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Here $F(x)\ge x$ for $x\ge0$, and $(.91595)^2<.839$. The symmetric pair of values $\pm x_0$ has $H(x_0)=b$, so its $H$-average equals that of $d$ and its transformed squared amplitude is $M$. Its edge energy is $x_0^2/b$. When $m=0$, the crossing identity gives $b=s$ and $x_0=\rho$, recovering a noised coordinate.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The calibrated edge cost

For $0\le v<F(1)^2$, define <a id="cen:phi"></a>$$\label{cen:phi}
 \Phi(v)=\frac{x^2}{H(x)}-v,
 \qquad x=F^{-1}(\sqrt v),\qquad
 j(v)=\begin{cases}\Phi(v)/v,&v>0,\\0,&v=0.\end{cases}$$ The endpoint value $F(1)$ is finite, since the integrand defining $F$ has an integrable singularity of order $3/4$ at $1$.

<a id="stmt-cen-edge"></a>
<div id="cen:edge" class="lemma">

<a id="cen:edge-bound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Calibrated edge comparison). *For every coordinate edge, $$\label{cen:edge-bound}
 D_i d\,D_i\!\left(\frac d{H(d)}\right)
 \ge (D_i g)^2+\Phi\bigl((D_i g)^2\bigr).$$ The functions $j$ and $\Phi$ are nonnegative, increasing, and convex. Furthermore, $\Phi'(v)/v$ is increasing for $v>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-edge"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-edge</summary>

<a id="cen:ell"></a>
*Proof.* Put $G=F(x)$ and $\ell(G)=\log F'(x)$. Direct differentiation gives $$\label{cen:ell}
 \ell'(G)=\frac{3x}{2\sqrt{H(x)}},\qquad
 \ell''(G)=\frac34\left(H(x)+\frac1{H(x)}\right),\qquad
 \ell'''(G)=\frac{3x^3}{4H(x)^{3/2}}.$$ Thus $\ell$ is even and convex, and $\ell''$ is increasing on the nonnegative half of its domain.

Consider an edge with distinct $g$-values $G_-<G_+$, and let $I=[G_-,G_+]$. Changing variables from $x$ to $G$ in the two differences shows that the ratio of its energy to $(D_i g)^2$ is $$\left(\frac1{|I|}\int_I e^{\ell(G)}\,dG\right)
 \left(\frac1{|I|}\int_I e^{-\ell(G)}\,dG\right)
 =\mathbb E\cosh\bigl(\ell(U)-\ell(V)\bigr),$$ where $U,V$ are independent uniform points of $I$. For a fixed separation $\delta=|U-V|$, the midpoint is uniform on the interval of length $|I|-\delta$ with the same center as $I$. The function $$A_\delta(c)=\ell(c+\delta/2)-\ell(c-\delta/2)$$ is odd, and is increasing because $A_\delta'(c)=\ell'(c+\delta/2)-\ell'(c-\delta/2)\ge0$. Consequently $\cosh A_\delta(c)$ is even and increasing in $|c|$. The integral of any such function over an interval of fixed length is minimized by centering that interval at zero: for positive center $c$, the derivative of the integral is the value at the right endpoint minus the value at the left endpoint, which is nonnegative. The domain of $\ell$ is the symmetric interval $(-F(1),F(1))$. Moving the center of $I$ toward zero while keeping its length fixed keeps $I$, and hence every conditional midpoint interval, in this domain. Applying the preceding fixed-length integral comparison conditionally on $\delta$ proves that the displayed product of averages is minimized when $I$ is centered at zero.

If that centered interval has endpoints $\pm a$, its corresponding $d$-values are $\pm x$, where $F(x)=a$. Its energy is exactly $x^2/H(x)=a^2+\Phi(a^2)$. Since $a^2=(D_i g)^2$, this proves <a href="#cen:edge-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:edge-bound">[cen:edge-bound]</a>. A zero difference gives the same statement directly.

The symmetric-interval computation also gives, for $v>0$, <a id="cen:j-average"></a>$$\label{cen:j-average}
 j(v)=\frac14\int_{-1}^1\int_{-1}^1
 \left[\cosh\bigl(\ell(\sqrt v\,a)-\ell(\sqrt v\,c)\bigr)-1\right]
 \,da\,dc.$$ To prove convexity of the integrand in $v$, use evenness of $\ell$ and $\cosh$ to replace $(a,c)$ by numbers $A\ge C\ge0$. For $B(v)=\ell(A\sqrt v)-\ell(C\sqrt v)$, differentiation gives $$B'(v)=\frac{z_A\ell'(z_A)-z_C\ell'(z_C)}{2v},\qquad
 B''(v)=\frac{Q(z_A)-Q(z_C)}{4v^2},
 \quad z_A=A\sqrt v,\ z_C=C\sqrt v,$$ where $Q(z)=z(z\ell''(z)-\ell'(z))$. The function $z\ell'(z)$ is increasing on $z\ge0$, and $$Q'(z)=z\ell''(z)-\ell'(z)+z^2\ell'''(z)\ge0.$$ Indeed, $\ell'(0)=0$ and increasing $\ell''$ imply $\ell'(z)\le z\ell''(z)$, and <a href="#cen:ell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:ell">[cen:ell]</a> gives $\ell'''(z)\ge0$. Thus $B$ is nonnegative, increasing, and convex. Its composition with $\cosh-1$ has the same properties. Averaging proves these properties for $j$, including its continuous extension $j(0)=0$. Now $\Phi(v)=vj(v)$ gives $\Phi'=j+vj'\ge0$ and $\Phi''=2j'+vj''\ge0$. Finally, convexity and $j(0)=0$ imply that $j(v)/v$ is increasing, while $j'$ is increasing. Therefore $$\frac{\Phi'(v)}v=\frac{j(v)}v+j'(v)$$ is increasing as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="cen:pdelta"></a>
Define the slope and energy excess at the calibration by $$\label{cen:pdelta}
 p_*:=\Phi'(M)=\frac{1+b^2}{2\alpha b^{3/2}}-1,\qquad
 \Delta:=M+\Phi(M)-K=(s-b)\left(1+\frac1{bs}\right).$$ The first identity follows by differentiating $x^2/H(x)$ with respect to $F(x)^2$; the second uses $M+\Phi(M)=(1-b^2)/b$ and $K=(1-s^2)/s$. In particular, $p_*,\Delta\ge0$.

<a id="stmt-cen-constants"></a>
<div id="cen:constants" class="lemma">

<a id="cen:four-constants"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Calibration bounds). *At a crossing in the central band, $$\label{cen:four-constants}
 \frac{\Phi(M)}M<.0063,\qquad p_*<.019,\qquad
 \frac{p_*}M<.0532,\qquad \frac\Delta M<.008.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-constants"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-constants</summary>

*Proof.* As $b$ decreases, $M$ increases. Lemma <a href="#cen:edge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:edge">11</a> therefore reduces the first three upper bounds to $b_0=.839$. Set $\alpha_0=F(\sqrt{1-b_0^2})/\sqrt{1-b_0^2}$. At this endpoint the positive binomial series, integrated term by term, gives $$\alpha_0\ge\sum_{j=0}^{5}
 \frac{(3/4)_j(1-b_0^2)^j}{j!(2j+1)}>a_0:=1.08834,
 \qquad \sqrt{b_0}>y_0:=.91595,$$ where $(3/4)_0=1$ and $(3/4)_j=(3/4)(7/4)\cdots(3/4+j-1)$. Using $\Phi(M)/M=1/(b\alpha^2)-1$ and <a href="#cen:pdelta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:pdelta">[cen:pdelta]</a>, exact rational comparisons give $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac1{b_0a_0^2}-1&<.006257<.0063,\\
 P_0:=\frac{1+b_0^2}{2a_0b_0y_0}-1&<.018641<.019,\\
 \frac{P_0}{(1-b_0^2)a_0^2}&<.053154<.0532.
\end{aligned}$$ For the last bound, <a href="#cen:domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:domain">[cen:domain]</a> and $m^2\le r^4/4$ give $$\frac\Delta M\le
 \frac{r^3}{4(1+h)}\left(1+\frac1{bs}\right)
 <\frac{R_0^3}{4(1.999)}
       \left(1+\frac1{(.839)(.84)}\right)<.007720<.008.$$ The numerical comparisons above are exact rational inequalities. The four bounds in <a href="#cen:four-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:four-constants">[cen:four-constants]</a> are also verified by the central-band certificate described in Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">9</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Reduction to variance and first-level estimates

<a id="cen:spectral-energy"></a>
<a id="cen:tangent-energy"></a>
Let $a_i$ be the degree-one Fourier coefficients of $g$, and put $$q=\sum_i a_i^2,\qquad l=\max_i a_i^2,\qquad H_0=\operatorname{Var}g-q.$$ Thus $H_0$ is the Fourier weight above degree one. Counting degrees gives $\sum_i\mathbb E(D_i g)^2\ge\operatorname{Var}g+H_0$. All arguments of $\Phi$ used below belong to its open domain: since the cube is finite, $G_{\max}:=\max|g|<F(1)$; every half-difference $|D_i g|$ and coefficient $|a_i|$ is at most $G_{\max}$, while $M=F(x_0)^2<F(1)^2$ because $b>0$. In a coordinate attaining $l$, Jensen’s inequality and monotonicity of $\Phi$ give $\mathbb E\Phi((D_i g)^2)\ge\Phi(\mathbb E(D_i g)^2)\ge\Phi(l)$. Lemma <a href="#cen:edge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:edge">11</a> and the tangent inequality for $\Phi$ at $M$ yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E(d)&\ge\operatorname{Var}g+H_0+\Phi(l)\label{cen:spectral-energy}\\
 &\ge K+(1+p_*)(\operatorname{Var}g-M)+(1-p_*)H_0
             -p_*(q-l)+\Delta.\label{cen:tangent-energy}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="cen:variance-gain"></a>
Here $q-l$ is the degree-one weight outside a largest coordinate. Put <a id="cen:V0"></a>$$\label{cen:V0}
 V_0=\mathbb E(g^2-M)^2,\qquad C_0=.034.$$ We will prove $$\label{cen:variance-gain}
 \operatorname{Var}g-M\ge C_0V_0-\frac\Delta{1+p_*},$$ with strict inequality unless $m=0$ and $g^2=M$ everywhere. Substituting this estimate into <a href="#cen:tangent-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:tangent-energy">[cen:tangent-energy]</a> cancels $\Delta$. We will then control the remaining negative term by proving $$p_*(q-l)\le(1+p_*)C_0V_0+(1-p_*)H_0.$$ These two bounds give $\mathcal E(d)>K$ whenever the variance inequality is strict. We first prove that inequality, then establish the first-level comparison and treat the remaining equality case.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A variance gain around the calibration

<a id="cen:k-ode"></a>
Define, on $0\le t<1$, $$k(t)=\frac{F(\sqrt{1-t^4})}{\sqrt{1-t^4}},\qquad k(1)=1.$$ Writing $u=\sqrt{H(d)}$, one has $g=k(u)d$, $k(y)=\alpha$, and $$\label{cen:k-ode}
 k'(t)=-\frac{2(1-t^3k(t))}{1-t^4},\qquad
 g^2-M=-4\int_y^u k(t)\,dt.$$ Both identities follow by differentiation; the second also holds at $u=1$ by continuity.

<a id="stmt-cen-k-bounds"></a>
<div id="cen:k-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="cen:k-estimates"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Bounds for the comparison function). *For $0\le t\le1$, $$\label{cen:k-estimates}
 2-t+\tfrac12(1-t)^2\le k(t)\le2-t+\tfrac34(1-t)^2,
 \qquad
 1\le-k'(t)\le\min\{2,1+\tfrac32(1-t)\}.$$ At $t=1$, the derivative is understood as its left limit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-k-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-k-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* For $P_c(t)=2-t+c(1-t)^2$, define the differential residual $$E_c(t)=-(1-t^4)P_c'(t)-2(1-t^3P_c(t)).$$ Expansion yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{E_{1/2}(t)}{(1-t)^2}&=t(2t^2-2t-1)\le0,\\
 \frac{E_{3/4}(t)}{(1-t)^2}
 &=\tfrac12(2t+1)(3t^2-3t+1)>0.
\end{aligned}$$ The first sign uses $2t^2-2t-1\le-1$; for the second, $3t^2-3t+1=3(t-1/2)^2+1/4$. To justify backward comparison despite the singular endpoint, fix $t<v<1$. The error $e=k-P_c$ satisfies $e'-2t^3e/(1-t^4)=E_c/(1-t^4)$, whence $$e(t)=\sqrt{\frac{1-v^4}{1-t^4}}\,e(v)
 -\int_t^v\sqrt{\frac{1-z^4}{1-t^4}}
                   \frac{E_c(z)}{1-z^4}\,dz.$$ Both $k(v)$ and $P_c(v)$ tend to $1$. The first term therefore vanishes as $v\uparrow1$, and the integral converges. Its sign proves the two polynomial comparisons in <a href="#cen:k-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:k-estimates">[cen:k-estimates]</a>.

Substitute these comparisons into <a href="#cen:k-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:k-ode">[cen:k-ode]</a>. The sufficient numerators for the three derivative inequalities are, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 2(1-t^3P_{3/4})-(1-t^4)
   &=\tfrac12(1-t)^2(-3t^3+6t^2+4t+2),\\
 2(1-t^4)-2(1-t^3P_{1/2})
   &=t^3(t-5)(t-1),\\
 \tfrac{5-3t}{2}(1-t^4)-2(1-t^3P_{1/2})
   &=\tfrac12(1-t)^2(5t^3-3t^2-t+1).
\end{aligned}$$ The first two are nonnegative on $[0,1]$. For the last cubic, on $[0,.6]$ use $5t^3-3t^2+.16=(5t+1)(5t-2)^2/25\ge0$, leaving at least $.24$. On $[.6,1]$ write the cubic as $t^2(5t-3)+(1-t)\ge0$. This proves the derivative bounds for $t<1$. The Taylor expansion $F(x)/x=1+x^2/4+O(x^4)$ at $x=0$ shows that $k'(1)=-1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="cen:variance-rearrange"></a>
To estimate the variance of $g$, separate its mean from the known mean $\mathbb Ed=m$ by subtracting $\alpha d$. Define <a id="cen:RD"></a>$$\label{cen:RD}
 R=g-\alpha d,\qquad
 D(u)=g^2-M-R^2+\frac{2\alpha}{y}(u^2-y^2).$$ The right side defining $D$ depends only on $u$, because $R^2=(1-u^4)(k(u)-\alpha)^2$. The added multiple of $u^2-y^2$ has zero expectation. Since $\mathbb Eu^2=b=y^2$, $\mathbb Eg=\mathbb ER+\alpha m$, and $(\mathbb ER)^2\le\mathbb ER^2$, one obtains $$\label{cen:variance-rearrange}
 \operatorname{Var}g-M\ge\mathbb E\bigl[D-2\alpha m|R|\bigr]-\alpha^2m^2.$$

<a id="stmt-cen-pointwise"></a>
<div id="cen:pointwise" class="lemma">

<a id="cen:pointwise-bound"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Pointwise remainder bound). *Under the central-band crossing hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:central">10</a>, for every $0\le u\le1$, $$\label{cen:pointwise-bound}
 D(u)-2\alpha m|R|+.88m^2
 \ge C_0(g^2-M)^2+.20(u^2-y^2)^2.$$ The inequality is strict whenever $u\ne y$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-pointwise"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-pointwise</summary>

<a id="cen:D-integral"></a>
<a id="cen:D-lower"></a>
*Proof.* The integral identity in <a href="#cen:k-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:k-ode">[cen:k-ode]</a> gives exactly $$\label{cen:D-integral}
 D(u)=\frac{2\alpha}{y}(u-y)^2
       +4\int_u^y(k(t)-\alpha)\,dt
       -(1-u^4)(k(u)-\alpha)^2.$$ Let $L=|u-y|$ and $\lambda=|k(u)-k(y)|$. Monotonicity of $k$ makes the integral nonnegative. A function with slope of magnitude at most $2$ needs length at least $\lambda/2$ to rise from $0$ to $\lambda$; the triangle of that base and height is a lower bound for its integral. Thus $$4\int_u^y(k(t)-\alpha)\,dt\ge\lambda^2.$$ The lower derivative bound in Lemma <a href="#cen:k-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:k-bounds">13</a> gives $\lambda\ge L$, so <a href="#cen:D-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:D-integral">[cen:D-integral]</a> implies $$\label{cen:D-lower}
 D(u)\ge\left(\frac{2\alpha}{y}+u^4\right)L^2.$$

<a id="cen:BJ"></a>
<a id="cen:pointwise-reduction"></a>
<a id="cen:polynomial-signs"></a>
For explicit estimates, put $a=1-u$, $c=1-y$, and $$\begin{aligned}
 A_-&=1+c+\tfrac12c^2,& A_+&=1+c+\tfrac34c^2,\\
 B&=1+\tfrac12(a+c)+\tfrac14(a^2+ac+c^2),&
 J&=1+\tfrac34(a+c).
\end{aligned}$$ Lemma <a href="#cen:k-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:k-bounds">13</a>, and its averages over the interval joining $u$ and $y$, give $$\label{cen:BJ}
 A_-\le\alpha\le A_+,\qquad
 |g^2-M|\le4BL,\qquad
 |R|\le J L\sqrt{1-u^4}.$$ In fact $B$ is the average of the upper quadratic bound for $k$, and $J$ is the average of the upper linear bound for $-k'$. Define <a id="cen:S"></a>$$\label{cen:S}
 S(u,y)=2A_-+y\bigl(u^4-16C_0B^2-.20(u+y)^2\bigr).$$ By <a href="#cen:D-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:D-lower">[cen:D-lower]</a>–<a href="#cen:BJ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:BJ">[cen:BJ]</a>, the left side minus the right side of <a href="#cen:pointwise-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:pointwise-bound">[cen:pointwise-bound]</a> is at least $$\label{cen:pointwise-reduction}
 \frac{L^2}{y}S
 -2A_+JmL\sqrt{1-u^4}+.88m^2.$$ If $u\le .8$, then $L=y-u>0$. Since $b\le s$ and $2m\le r^2$, $$2m\le r^2\le(1-b^2)^2=(1-y^4)^2.$$ Dropping $.88m^2$ and bounding $\sqrt{1-u^4}\le1$ makes <a href="#cen:pointwise-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:pointwise-reduction">[cen:pointwise-reduction]</a> at least $LP_1/y$, where <a id="cen:P1"></a>$$\label{cen:P1}
 P_1(u,y)=(y-u)S-A_+J(1-y^4)^2y.$$ If $u\ge .8$ and $L>0$, the elementary inequality $$2A_+JmL\sqrt{1-u^4}
 \le .88m^2+\frac{(A_+J)^2L^2(1-u^4)}{.88}$$ makes the same expression at least $L^2P_2/(.88y)$, where <a id="cen:P2"></a>$$\label{cen:P2}
 P_2(u,y)=.88S-(A_+J)^2(1-u^4)y.$$ It therefore suffices, when $L>0$, to prove the polynomial signs $$\label{cen:polynomial-signs}
 \begin{aligned}
 P_1(u,y)&>0 &&(0\le u\le .8,\ .915\le y\le1),\\
 P_2(u,y)&>0 &&(.8\le u\le1,\ .915\le y\le1).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here is a finite rational certificate for these signs, using the Bernstein coefficient formula of Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">9</a>. Map each stated rectangle affinely to $[0,1]^2$, with the $u$ coordinate first. If the resulting power coefficients are $c_{ab}$, its Bernstein coefficient of index $(i,j)$ and order $(N_1,N_2)$ is <a id="cen:bernstein-formula"></a>$$\label{cen:bernstein-formula}
 \beta_{ij}=\sum_{a\le i,\,b\le j}c_{ab}
       \frac{\binom ia}{\binom{N_1}a}
       \frac{\binom jb}{\binom{N_2}b}.$$ The orders and row minima are as follows; the final column records $\lfloor1000\min_j\beta_{ij}\rfloor$ for successive $i$.

<div class="center">

| Polynomial | Order $(N_1,N_2)$ | Scaled row minima               |
|:----------:|:-----------------:|:--------------------------------|
|   $P_1$    |     $(5,12)$      | $134,353,365,278,214,46$        |
|   $P_2$    |      $(6,7)$      | $179,364,559,765,983,1214,1457$ |

</div>

Thus every coefficient is strictly positive. Bernstein basis functions are nonnegative and sum to one on the unit square, proving <a href="#cen:polynomial-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:polynomial-signs">[cen:polynomial-signs]</a> on the closed rectangles. The formula and the explicit polynomials specify all arithmetic in the table; the executable certificate is `verification/central_band.py`. Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">9</a> gives the general certificate convention.

The two lower bounds above are therefore strictly positive whenever $L>0$. Finally, when $u=y$, all terms in <a href="#cen:pointwise-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:pointwise-bound">[cen:pointwise-bound]</a> vanish except $.88m^2$, which is nonnegative. This also proves the asserted strictness. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cen-strict-variance"></a>
<div id="cen:strict-variance" class="lemma">

<a id="cen:positive-margin"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Strict variance gain). *Inequality <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a> holds. If $m>0$, the stronger bound $$\label{cen:positive-margin}
 \operatorname{Var}g-M>C_0V_0-\frac\Delta{1+p_*}+.016m^2$$ holds. If $m=0$, <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a> is strict unless $g^2=M$ at every point of the cube.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-strict-variance"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-strict-variance</summary>

<a id="cen:T-ratio"></a>
*Proof.* Write $T=\mathbb E(u^2-y^2)^2=\mathbb E(H(d)-b)^2$. Since $\mathbb Ed=m$ and $H(d)^2=1-d^2$, $T=h^2-b^2-\operatorname{Var}d$.
