Lemma <a href="#lem:fourier-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fourier-bounds">9</a>, using $w\le1-m$, therefore gives $$T\ge(1-r^2)h^2-b^2-rs^2(1-m).$$ The crossing identity implies $sh-b=(1-s)(1-h)\ge0$. Substituting $b^2\le s^2h^2$ proves <a id="cen:T-lower"></a>$$\label{cen:T-lower}
 T\ge rs^2(m-m^2).$$ For $m>0$ this yields $$\label{cen:T-ratio}
 \frac T{m^2}\ge s^2\left(\frac2r-r\right)
 \ge(1-R_0)\left(\frac2{R_0}-R_0\right)>4.5.$$ Both positive factors in the middle expression decrease with $r$ on $0<r\le R_0$, and the final inequality is rational.

<a id="cen:delta-cancel"></a>
We also need to compare the calibration loss with $\alpha^2m^2$. The upper bound for $k(y)$ gives $\alpha<1.1$ and $$\alpha y\le(1-c)(1+c+\tfrac34c^2)
       =1-\tfrac14c^2-\tfrac34c^3\le1,
 \qquad c=1-y\in[0,.085].$$ Using <a href="#cen:pdelta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:pdelta">[cen:pdelta]</a> and $s-b=m^2/(1+h)$, for $m>0$ we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac\Delta{(1+p_*)m^2}
 &=\frac\alpha y\,\frac2{1+h}
       \left(1-\frac{s-b}{s(1+b^2)}\right)\label{cen:delta-cancel}\\
 &>\alpha^2-\frac{(1.1)^2(.001)}{.84}
 >\alpha^2-.004.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ In the first factor of <a href="#cen:delta-cancel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:delta-cancel">[cen:delta-cancel]</a>, $\alpha/y\ge\alpha^2$; also $2/(1+h)\ge1$. The subtracted relative loss is less than $.001/.84$, by <a href="#cen:domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:domain">[cen:domain]</a>, which proves the next line.

Averaging <a href="#cen:pointwise-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:pointwise-bound">[cen:pointwise-bound]</a> and applying <a href="#cen:variance-rearrange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-rearrange">[cen:variance-rearrange]</a> now gives $$\operatorname{Var}g-M\ge C_0V_0+.20T-(\alpha^2+.88)m^2.$$ For $m>0$, insert <a href="#cen:T-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:T-ratio">[cen:T-ratio]</a> and <a href="#cen:delta-cancel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:delta-cancel">[cen:delta-cancel]</a>. The remaining strict margin is $.20(4.5)-.88-.004=.016$, proving <a href="#cen:positive-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:positive-margin">[cen:positive-margin]</a>. For $m=0$ one has $b=s$ and $\Delta=0$; the same averaged inequality proves <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a>. If any cube point has $u\ne y$, Lemma <a href="#cen:pointwise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:pointwise">14</a> makes the averaged inequality strict. Since $k>0$ in <a href="#cen:k-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:k-ode">[cen:k-ode]</a>, the condition $u=y$ is equivalent to $g^2=M$, completing the strictness statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Concentration of the first Fourier level

<details id="proof-stmt-cen-strict-variance-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cen-strict-variance</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:central">10</a>.* Recall that $q$ is the total degree-one Fourier weight of $g$, $l$ is its largest coordinate weight, and $H_0=\operatorname{Var}g-q$ is its weight above degree one.

<a id="cen:resources"></a>
The resources available to control $q-l$ follow without losing the $\Delta$ term. Namely, <a href="#cen:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:crossing">[cen:crossing]</a>, <a href="#cen:spectral-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:spectral-energy">[cen:spectral-energy]</a>, and <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a> imply $$\label{cen:resources}
 H_0+C_0V_0\le\Phi(M)-\frac{p_*\Delta}{1+p_*}\le\Phi(M),
 \qquad
 q\ge M-\Phi(M)-\Delta>.985M>0.$$ Indeed, substituting the variance gain into the first energy bound gives $$H_0+C_0V_0\le K-M+\frac{\Delta}{1+p_*}-\Phi(l)
 =\Phi(M)-\frac{p_*\Delta}{1+p_*}-\Phi(l).$$ Since $\Phi(l)\ge0$, this proves the first resource bound. Combining it with the variance gain once more yields $$q\ge M-\Phi(M)+2C_0V_0+\frac{p_*-1}{1+p_*}\Delta
 \ge M-\Phi(M)-\Delta.$$ Thus the second inequality retains the full calibration loss; Lemma <a href="#cen:constants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:constants">12</a> gives $1-.0063-.008=.9857>.985$.

<a id="cen:spread"></a>
<a id="cen:quadratic"></a>
Let $$L_1(x)=\sum_i a_i x_i,\qquad P=L_1^2-q,\qquad
 W=\mathbb EP^2=2\left(q^2-\sum_i a_i^4\right).$$ Since $\sum_i a_i^4\le lq$, $$\label{cen:spread}
 q-l\le\frac W{2q}.$$ Write $g=\mu+L_1+Z$, with $\mu=\mathbb Eg$ and $Z$ containing only Fourier degrees at least two; thus $\mathbb EZ^2=H_0$. The degree-three part of $L_1P$ has coefficient $6a_i a_j a_k$ on $x_ix_jx_k$, so its squared norm is $$36\sum_{i<j<k}a_i^2a_j^2a_k^2\le3qW.$$ To see the last inequality, write $W=4\sum_{i<j}a_i^2a_j^2$: in $qW$ each distinct triple occurs three times, with total coefficient $12$, and all remaining terms are nonnegative. Only that degree-three part of $L_1P$ pairs with $Z$. Also $P\ge-q$, $\mathbb EP=\mathbb EL_1P=0$, and $\mathbb EL_1^2P=W$. Expanding $g^2P$ and applying Cauchy–Schwarz therefore gives $$\label{cen:quadratic}
 \sqrt{V_0W}\ge\mathbb E[g^2P]
 \ge W-(2\sqrt{3q}+2|\mu|)\sqrt{WH_0}-qH_0.$$ The first inequality uses $\mathbb E[g^2P]=\mathbb E[(g^2-M)P]$. For the last inequality the three error terms are $2\mathbb E(L_1PZ)$, $2\mu\mathbb E(PZ)$, and $\mathbb E(Z^2P)$, bounded below by $-2\sqrt{3qWH_0}$, $-2|\mu|\sqrt{WH_0}$, and $-qH_0$ respectively.

<a id="cen:mean-bound"></a>
The mean term is small. Lemma <a href="#cen:k-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:k-bounds">13</a> and <a href="#cen:k-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:k-ode">[cen:k-ode]</a> give $$|g-\alpha d|\le2|u-y|\le\tfrac12|g^2-M|.$$ Together with <a href="#cen:resources" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:resources">[cen:resources]</a> this proves $$\label{cen:mean-bound}
 \frac{|\mu|}{\sqrt q}
 \le\frac{m/x_0+\tfrac12\sqrt{.0063/C_0}}{\sqrt{.985}}
 \le\frac{R_0(.543)/2+.431/2}{.9924}<.298<.31.$$ Here $m/x_0\le r^{3/2}/2$, and the rational comparisons $(.543)^2>R_0$, $(.431)^2>.0063/.034$, and $(.9924)^2<.985$ justify all square-root bounds.

<a id="cen:W-bound"></a>
Set $A=\sqrt{V_0}$, $B_0=\sqrt{qH_0}$, and $z=\sqrt W$. Equations <a href="#cen:quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:quadratic">[cen:quadratic]</a> and <a href="#cen:mean-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:mean-bound">[cen:mean-bound]</a> imply $$z^2\le Az+(2\sqrt3+.62)B_0z+B_0^2.$$ Writing $c_0=2\sqrt3+.62$, the positive root of this quadratic is at most $A+4.32B_0$. Indeed, substituting that value in the polynomial $z^2-(A+c_0B_0)z-B_0^2$ gives $$(4.32-c_0)AB_0+(4.32^2-4.32c_0-1)B_0^2\ge0,$$ and the substituted value is beyond its vertex. Both coefficients are positive: $\sqrt3<1.73206$ gives $4.32>c_0$ and $4.32^2>1+4.32c_0$ by rational comparison. The degenerate cases $B_0=0$ or $W=0$ obey the same bound. Thus $$\label{cen:W-bound}
 \sqrt W\le\sqrt{V_0}+4.32\sqrt{qH_0}.$$

<a id="cen:absorption"></a>
The constants leave a strict margin for absorbing this spread: <a id="cen:absorption-constant"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{p_*}{(1+p_*)qC_0}
 +\frac{p_*(4.32)^2}{1-p_*}
 &<\frac{.0532}{(.985)(.034)}
       +\frac{.019(4.32)^2}{.981}\label{cen:absorption-constant}\\
 &<1.95<2.\notag
\end{aligned}$$ For clarity, the middle rational number is $444890776/228150625$, which is strictly less than $39/20$. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz, applied to <a href="#cen:W-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:W-bound">[cen:W-bound]</a>, now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{p_*W}{2q}
 &\le\frac12\left(
       \frac{p_*}{(1+p_*)qC_0}
       +\frac{p_*(4.32)^2}{1-p_*}\right)
       \bigl((1+p_*)C_0V_0+(1-p_*)H_0\bigr)\notag\\
 &\le(1+p_*)C_0V_0+(1-p_*)H_0.
 \label{cen:absorption}
\end{aligned}$$ All denominators are positive by <a href="#cen:resources" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:resources">[cen:resources]</a> and $0\le p_*<.019$.

If <a href="#cen:variance-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:variance-gain">[cen:variance-gain]</a> is strict, substitute it into <a href="#cen:tangent-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:tangent-energy">[cen:tangent-energy]</a>, then use <a href="#cen:spread" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:spread">[cen:spread]</a> and <a href="#cen:absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:absorption">[cen:absorption]</a>. The result is $$\mathcal E(d)>K+(1+p_*)C_0V_0+(1-p_*)H_0-p_*(q-l)\ge K,$$ contradicting <a href="#cen:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cen:crossing">[cen:crossing]</a>. Lemma <a href="#cen:strict-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cen:strict-variance">15</a> therefore leaves only $m=0$ and $g^2=M$ everywhere.

In this exceptional case $b=s$, so $x_0=\sqrt{1-s^2}=\rho$. Because $F$ is odd and strictly increasing, $g^2=M$ implies $|d|=\rho$ everywhere. If $W_k(f)$ denotes the Fourier weight of $f$ at degree $k$, Parseval and $m=0$ give $$\sum_{k\ge1}W_k(f)=1,\qquad
 \sum_{k\ge1}r^kW_k(f)=\mathbb Ed^2=r.$$ Subtracting the second identity from $r$ times the first gives $\sum_{k\ge2}(r-r^k)W_k(f)=0$. Every coefficient $r-r^k$ is positive for $0<r<1$, so all weights above degree one vanish. Hence $f=\sum_i c_i x_i$, with $\sum_i c_i^2=1$. The identity $f^2=1$ has degree-two coefficients $2c_ic_j=0$ for every $i\ne j$. At most one $c_i$ is nonzero, and its square is $1$. Thus $f$ is a signed coordinate, proving Proposition <a href="#prop:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:central">10</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:intermediate"></a>
### Intermediate biases

We retain the notation of Section <a href="#sec:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuation">4</a>. In particular, $21/25\le s<1$, $r=1-s^2$, $d=T_\rho f$, $m=\mathbb Ef\ge0$, $h=H(m)$, $b=\mathbb EH(d)$, and $g=F(d)$, where $F(0)=0$ and $F'=H^{-3/2}$. The first-level bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:fourier-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fourier-bounds">9</a> is denoted by $$w=\min\{2p,4\sqrt{3/2}\,p^{3/2}\},\qquad p=(1-m)/2.$$ All terminating decimals in this section denote exact rational numbers.

<a id="stmt-prop-intermediate"></a>
<div id="prop:intermediate" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (Exclusion of an intermediate-bias crossing). *Let $f$ be a nonconstant Boolean function and suppose $$\frac{r^2}{2}\le m\le\frac{37}{50},\qquad \frac{21}{25}\le s<1.$$ If $b=h-1+s$, then $\mathcal E(d)>r/s$. Consequently a crossing as in Lemma <a href="#lem:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:crossing">7</a> cannot occur in this range.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Parity and an energy reduction

<a id="mid:odd-cap"></a>
<a id="mid:parity-energy"></a>
For a function $u$ on the cube, write $$u_e(x)=\frac{u(x)+u(-x)}2,\qquad
 u_o(x)=\frac{u(x)-u(-x)}2.$$ These are the projections onto the even and odd Fourier degrees. Every nonconstant even degree is at least two, whereas an odd degree other than one is at least three. As in Lemma <a href="#lem:fourier-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fourier-bounds">9</a>, Parseval and $W_1(f)\le w$ give $$\label{mid:odd-cap}
 \operatorname{Var}(d_o)
 \le rW_1(f)+r^3\bigl(h^2-W_1(f)\bigr)
 \le r\bigl[(1-r^2)w+r^2h^2\bigr].$$ The edge comparison of Lemma <a href="#lem:basic-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:basic-energy">8</a>, followed by the same degree count for $g$, gives $$\label{mid:parity-energy}
 \mathcal E(d)\ge\sum_i\mathbb E(D_i g)^2
 \ge\operatorname{Var}(g)+\operatorname{Var}(g_e).$$

<a id="mid:V"></a>
<a id="mid:EV"></a>
<a id="mid:Psi"></a>
<a id="mid:energy-reduction"></a>
<a id="mid:target"></a>
To use the extra even-degree contribution, we compare $g=F(d)$ with its affine approximation at $m$. Set $v=1-s$ and $a=F'(m)=h^{-3/2}$. The tangent deficit of $H$ at $m$ is $$\label{mid:V}
 V=h-H(d)-\frac{m(d-m)}h.$$ Concavity gives $V\ge0$, and at a putative crossing, $$\label{mid:EV}
 \mathbb EV=h-b=v.$$ The variance expansion $$\operatorname{Var}(g)=\operatorname{Var}(g-ad)
       +2a\mathbb E\bigl[(d-m)(g-F(m))\bigr]-a^2\operatorname{Var}(d)$$ is valid without any assumption on $\mathbb Eg$. On the nonconstant even subspace, apply the identity $$\|u-v_0\|_2^2+\|u\|_2^2
 =2\|u-v_0/2\|_2^2+\|v_0\|_2^2/2$$ with $u=g_e-\mathbb Eg$ and $v_0=a(d_e-m)$. Dropping the odd part of $\operatorname{Var}(g-ad)$ then gives $$\operatorname{Var}(g-ad)+\operatorname{Var}(g_e)\ge\frac{a^2}{2}\operatorname{Var}(d_e).$$ This compensates for half the even part of the variance penalty. Define the remaining pointwise expression by $$\label{mid:Psi}
 \Psi=2a(d-m)\bigl(g-F(m)\bigr)
       -\frac{a^2}{2}(d-m)^2-3V.$$ Inserting the variance expansion into <a href="#mid:parity-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:parity-energy">[mid:parity-energy]</a> and using <a href="#mid:odd-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:odd-cap">[mid:odd-cap]</a> now gives $$\label{mid:energy-reduction}
 \mathcal E(d)\ge3v+\mathbb E\Psi-\frac{a^2}{2}\operatorname{Var}(d_o)
 \ge3v+\mathbb E\Psi-\frac r2 Q,
 \qquad Q=\frac{(1-r^2)w}{h^3}+\frac{r^2}{h}.$$ Define <a id="mid:Qstar"></a>$$\label{mid:Qstar}
 Q_* =\frac{2(2s-1)}{s(1+s)}
      =\frac6{1+s}-\frac2s.$$ Since $r=v(1+s)$, the identity $3v-rQ_*/2=r/s$ shows that the following strict inequality suffices: $$\label{mid:target}
 \mathbb E\Psi>\frac r2(Q-Q_*).$$

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Pointwise bounds and the bias margins

Near $m=r^2/2$, a positive multiple of $V^2$ supplies the needed gain because $\mathbb EV=v$. For $m\ge1/5$, the gap $Q_*-Q$ will instead allow a small negative multiple of $V$. We first use these estimates to prove Proposition <a href="#prop:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:intermediate">16</a>, then prove the estimates in angular coordinates.

<a id="stmt-mid-pointwise"></a>
<div id="mid:pointwise" class="lemma">

<a id="mid:pointwise-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 17**. *For every $-1<d<1$, with $V,\Psi$ defined by <a href="#mid:V" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:V">[mid:V]</a> and <a href="#mid:Psi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:Psi">[mid:Psi]</a>, one has $$\label{mid:pointwise-bounds}
 \begin{array}{ll}
 \Psi\ge(\frac12-2m^2)V^2,&0\le m\le\frac15,\\[2pt]
 \Psi\ge-\frac1{100}V,&\frac15\le m\le\frac23,\\[2pt]
 \Psi\ge-\frac1{50}V,&\frac23\le m\le\frac{37}{50}.
 \end{array}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mid-pointwise"><summary>Proof of stmt-mid-pointwise</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:intermediate">16</a>.* Assume $b=h-1+s$. Since $s\ge21/25$, <a id="mid:r-bound"></a>$$\label{mid:r-bound}
 r^2\le(184/625)^2<87/1000.$$ The first term of the bound on $w$, namely $w\le1-m$, gives <a id="mid:Qzero"></a>$$\label{mid:Qzero}
 Q\le Q_0:=\frac{1+r^2m}{h(1+m)}.$$

<a id="mid:small-margin-left"></a>
First suppose $r^2/2\le m\le1/5$. In particular $m>0$. On this interval, $$h\ge1-\frac{51}{100}m^2,$$ as follows by squaring: the difference of the squares is $m^2(1/50-2601m^2/10000)\ge0$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{1-Q_0}{m}
 &=\frac1h\left[\frac{1-r^2}{1+m}-\frac{1-h}{m}\right]
 \ge L(m):=\frac{913}{1000(1+m)}-\frac{51m}{100}.
 \label{mid:small-margin-left}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the bracket is positive, since it is at least $L(m)\ge L(1/5)=3953/6000>0$; thus the factor $1/h\ge1$ has been removed in the correct direction.

<a id="mid:small-margin-right"></a>
<a id="mid:small-final-margin"></a>
We compare $L(m)$ with $$\label{mid:small-margin-right}
 R(m,s):=\frac{2v}{1+s}\left(\frac{v}{sm}+2m\right).$$ For $m\le3/50$, the hypothesis $m\ge r^2/2$ gives $$R(m,s)\le\frac4{s(1+s)^3}+\frac{4vm}{1+s}
 \le\frac4{(21/25)(46/25)^3}
     +\frac{4(4/25)(3/50)}{46/25}.$$ For $3/50\le m\le1/5$, use instead $$R(m,s)\le
 \frac{2(4/25)^2}{(21/25)(46/25)(3/50)}
 +\frac{4(4/25)(1/5)}{46/25}.$$ Since $L$ decreases, the exact comparisons are $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 \text{range of }m & \text{lower bound for }L(m)
                    &\text{upper bound for }R(m,s)\\ \hline
 \rule{0pt}{22pt}(0,3/50] & \dfrac{220141}{265000}>\dfrac45
          & \dfrac{10032241}{12775350}<\dfrac45\\[7pt]
 [3/50,1/5]&\dfrac{3953}{6000}>\dfrac{16}{25}
           &\dfrac{4504}{7245}<\dfrac{16}{25}.
\end{array}$$ Equations <a href="#mid:small-margin-left" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:small-margin-left">[mid:small-margin-left]</a>–<a href="#mid:small-margin-right" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:small-margin-right">[mid:small-margin-right]</a> therefore give $(1-Q)/m>R(m,s)$. Since $$1-Q_* =\frac{v(2-s)}{s(1+s)},$$ rearranging this strict inequality yields $$\label{mid:small-final-margin}
 \frac{1+s}{2v}(Q-Q_*)<\frac12-2m^2.$$ The first line of Lemma <a href="#mid:pointwise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mid:pointwise">17</a>, Jensen’s inequality, and <a href="#mid:EV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:EV">[mid:EV]</a> give $$\mathbb E\Psi\ge(\tfrac12-2m^2)\mathbb EV^2
           \ge(\tfrac12-2m^2)v^2
           >\frac r2(Q-Q_*),$$ where the last step uses $r=v(1+s)$ and <a href="#mid:small-final-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:small-final-margin">[mid:small-final-margin]</a>.

Next suppose $1/5\le m\le2/3$. For fixed $a_0=r^2\le87/1000$, $$(\log Q_0)'=\frac{a_0}{1+a_0m}+\frac{2m-1}{1-m^2},\qquad
 (\log Q_0)''=-\frac{a_0^2}{(1+a_0m)^2}
       +\frac{2(m^2-m+1)}{(1-m^2)^2}>0.$$ For the last sign, the positive term is at least $3/2$, and $a_0^2<1$. Convexity bounds $Q_0$ by its endpoint maximum; it also increases with $a_0$. At $a_0=87/1000$, the two endpoint values are $$Q_0(1/5)=\frac{5087}{2400\sqrt6}<\frac{433}{500}=.866,
 \qquad
 Q_0(2/3)=\frac{4761}{2500\sqrt5}<\frac{213}{250}=.852.$$ The strict comparisons follow respectively from $$25435^2<6\cdot10392^2,\qquad 4761^2<5\cdot2130^2.$$ Thus $Q<.866$ throughout this range.

Finally suppose $2/3\le m\le37/50$. The second term of the bound on $w$ gives <a id="mid:Qhigh"></a>$$\label{mid:Qhigh}
 Q\le R_0(m,a_0):=
 (1-a_0)\frac{\sqrt3}{(1+m)^{3/2}}+\frac{a_0}{\sqrt{1-m^2}},
 \qquad a_0=r^2\le87/1000.$$ The coefficient of $a_0$ is positive, because $(1+m)^2>3(1-m)$ on this interval. Hence it suffices to take $a_0=87/1000$. Moreover, $$\frac{\partial R_0}{\partial m}
 =-\frac{3(1-a_0)\sqrt3}{2(1+m)^{5/2}}
   +\frac{a_0m}{(1-m^2)^{3/2}}<0.$$ Here is a rational comparison proving the last sign on the entire interval. Since $h>2/3$, the positive term is less than $(87/1000)(37/50)(27/8)<1/4$. Since $\sqrt3>5/3$, $(1+m)^{5/2}<6$, and $1-a_0\ge913/1000$, the magnitude of the negative term is greater than $4565/12000>1/3$. At the left endpoint, $$R_0(2/3,87/1000)=\frac{4761}{2500\sqrt5}<.852,$$ so $Q<.852$ in the final range.

To finish both remaining ranges, observe that $$Q_*'(s)=\frac{2+4s(1-s)}{s^2(1+s)^2}>0,
 \qquad Q_*(21/25)=\frac{425}{483}>.879,
 \qquad \frac{1+s}{2}\ge\frac{23}{25}=.92.$$ The exact positive margins after the respective pointwise losses are $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{1+s}{2}(Q_*-Q)-\frac1{100}
 &>.92(.879-.866)-.01=\frac{49}{25000}>0,
       &&1/5\le m\le2/3,\\
 \frac{1+s}{2}(Q_*-Q)-\frac1{50}
 &>.92(.879-.852)-.02=\frac{121}{25000}>0,
       &&2/3\le m\le37/50.
\end{aligned}$$ By the appropriate line of Lemma <a href="#mid:pointwise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mid:pointwise">17</a>, $\mathbb E\Psi\ge-\varepsilon\mathbb EV=-\varepsilon v$; the last inequalities and $r=v(1+s)$ therefore imply <a href="#mid:target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:target">[mid:target]</a>. All three ranges give $\mathcal E(d)>r/s$ by <a href="#mid:energy-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:energy-reduction">[mid:energy-reduction]</a>, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Proof of the pointwise comparison

<details id="proof-stmt-mid-pointwise-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-mid-pointwise</summary>

<a id="mid:angular-identities"></a>
*Proof of Lemma <a href="#mid:pointwise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mid:pointwise">17</a>.* Introduce the midpoint and signed half-difference of the two angles: $$\beta=\arcsin m,\qquad t=\frac{\arcsin d-\beta}{2},\qquad
 c=t+\beta.$$ Then $|c|+|t|=\max\{|\arcsin d|,|\beta|\}<\pi/2$. The case $t=0$ gives $d=m$ and $V=\Psi=0$; hence assume $t\ne0$. Elementary angle identities give $$d-m=2\cos c\sin t,\qquad md+hH(d)=\cos(2t).$$ Changing variables $z=\sin\theta$ in the integral defining $F$ gives, with the orientation of the integral retained if $t<0$, $$g-F(m)=\int_{c-t}^{c+t}\frac{d\theta}{\sqrt{\cos\theta}}.$$ Normalize this integral by setting $$T=\frac mh,\qquad q=\frac{\cos c}{h}=\cos t-T\sin t>0,
 \qquad G=\tan c.$$ It follows that $$\label{mid:angular-identities}
 V=\frac{2\sin^2t}{h},\qquad
 \frac{\Psi}{V}=4\sqrt q\,A-q^2-3,
 \qquad
 A=\frac{t}{\sin t}\frac12\int_{-1}^1
       (\cos(tu)-G\sin(tu))^{-1/2}\,du.$$

<a id="mid:Taylor"></a>
<a id="mid:bracket"></a>
To bound $A$ from below, we keep the first three terms of a positive expansion. If $y=\sin^2(tu)$, symmetry in $u$ expresses the averaged integrand as $$\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{\binom{4j}{2j}}{16^j}
     G^{2j}y^j(1-y)^{-j-1/4}.$$ This expansion is convergent because $|G\tan(tu)|<1$, and its power coefficients in $y$ are nonnegative. Keeping the terms through degree two therefore gives the lower bound $$1+\left(\frac14+\frac{3G^2}{8}\right)y
  +\left(\frac5{32}+\frac{15G^2}{32}
                 +\frac{35G^4}{128}\right)y^2.$$ Concavity of sine on $[0,\pi/2]$ implies $\sin^2(tu)\ge u^2\sin^2t$. Averaging and writing $Y=\sin^2t$, we obtain $$\frac12\int_{-1}^1(\cos(tu)-G\sin(tu))^{-1/2}\,du
 \ge1+\left(\frac1{12}+\frac{G^2}{8}\right)Y
       +\left(\frac1{32}+\frac{3G^2}{32}
                       +\frac{7G^4}{128}\right)Y^2.$$ Multiply by the positive-series bound $t/\sin t\ge1+Y/6+3Y^2/40$, and discard terms of degree at least three. The result is $$\label{mid:Taylor}
 \frac{4(A-1)}{\sin^2t}\ge P_0,
 \qquad
 P_0=1+\frac{G^2}{2}
 +\sin^2t\left(\frac{173}{360}+\frac{11G^2}{24}
                                  +\frac{7G^4}{32}\right).$$ To express the remaining terms rationally, put $x=\tan(t/2)$. Then $$q-1=-\sin t(T+x),\qquad
 4\sqrt q-q^2-3
 =-(q-1)^2\left(1+\frac2{(1+\sqrt q)^2}\right).$$ Equations <a href="#mid:angular-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:angular-identities">[mid:angular-identities]</a> and <a href="#mid:Taylor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:Taylor">[mid:Taylor]</a> therefore imply $$\label{mid:bracket}
 \frac{\Psi}{V}\ge\sin^2t
 \left[\sqrt q\,P_0
 -(T+x)^2\left(1+\frac2{(1+\sqrt q)^2}\right)\right].$$

We now bound these square-root coefficients on the required angular domains.

For $m\le1/5$, $T\le1/\sqrt{24}<21/100$ and $\beta<21/100$. For $m\le37/50$, $\beta<21/25$. These bounds on $\beta$ follow from $\sin u\ge u-u^3/6$ at the respective rational values. Moreover, $$-\frac{\pi/2+\beta}{4}<\frac t2
 <\frac{\pi}{8},\qquad \tan(\pi/8)=\sqrt2-1<\frac{21}{50}.$$ Using $\pi<22/7$, the lower angle in absolute value is less than $9/20$ in the first range, and less than $603/1000$ in the second. The bounds $$\tan(9/20)<1/2,\qquad \tan(603/1000)<7/10$$ follow by dividing $\sin u\le u-u^3/6+u^5/120$ by $\cos u\ge1-u^2/2>0$; the resulting comparisons are rational. Thus $m\le1/5$ gives $0\le T\le21/100$ and $|x|\le1/2$, whereas the two larger ranges have $-7/10\le x\le21/50$. The corresponding bounds on $T$ are $$T\le2/\sqrt5<9/10\quad(m\le2/3),\qquad
 T\le37/\sqrt{1131}<111/100\quad(m\le37/50).$$

<a id="mid:root-identity"></a>
On the small rectangle, $q=(1-x^2-2Tx)/(1+x^2)\ge(27/50)/(5/4)=54/125=.432$, so $\sqrt q>13/20$. The exact identity $$\label{mid:root-identity}
 \sqrt q=1+\frac{q-1}{2}
          -\frac{(q-1)^2}{2(1+\sqrt q)^2}$$ and the rational comparisons $$\frac{200}{1089}<\frac{19}{100},\qquad
 \frac{1889}{1089}<\frac74$$ give $$\sqrt q\ge1+\frac{q-1}{2}-\frac{19}{100}(q-1)^2,
 \qquad 1+\frac2{(1+\sqrt q)^2}<\frac74.$$

For the two larger ranges we use a bound on the actual angular domain: $$q\ge\frac{\cos((\pi/2+\beta)/2)}h
   =\frac1{\sqrt{2(1+m)}}
   \ge\frac5{\sqrt{87}}>\frac{53}{100}.$$ In particular $\sqrt q>18/25$, and $$\frac{625}{3698}<\frac{17}{100},\qquad
 \frac{3099}{1849}<\frac{42}{25}.$$ Equation <a href="#mid:root-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:root-identity">[mid:root-identity]</a> therefore gives $$\sqrt q\ge1+\frac{q-1}{2}-\frac{17}{100}(q-1)^2,
 \qquad 1+\frac2{(1+\sqrt q)^2}<\frac{42}{25}.$$

<a id="mid:polynomial-variables"></a>
<a id="mid:R"></a>
<a id="mid:positive-denominators"></a>
<a id="mid:small-sign"></a>
<a id="mid:large-sign"></a>
These quadratic lower bounds for $\sqrt q$ make the bracket in <a href="#mid:bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:bracket">[mid:bracket]</a> rational in $T,x$. We clear its positive denominators using the polynomials <a id="mid:P"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &D=1+x^2,\qquad N=1-x^2-2Tx,\qquad
 z=2x,\qquad k=2x+T(1-x^2),
 \label{mid:polynomial-variables}\\
 &P=D^2\left(N^4+\frac{k^2N^2}{2}\right)
 +z^2\left(\frac{173N^4}{360}+\frac{11k^2N^2}{24}
                              +\frac{7k^4}{32}\right),
 \label{mid:P}\\
 &R_e=D^2+\frac{(N-D)D}{2}-e(N-D)^2.
 \label{mid:R}
\end{aligned}$$ At the angular parameters, $$\label{mid:positive-denominators}
 \begin{gathered}
 q=\frac ND,\qquad G=\frac kN,\qquad
 \sin t=\frac zD,\qquad P_0=\frac{P}{D^2N^4},\\
 D>0,\qquad N>0,\qquad P>0.
 \end{gathered}$$ The needed polynomial signs are $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal S(T,x)
 &:=R_{19/100}P-D^4N^4
       \left[1-\frac{33}{10}T^2+\frac74(T+x)^2\right]\ge0
 \label{mid:small-sign}\\[-3pt]
 &\hspace{35mm}(0\le T\le21/100,\ |x|\le1/2),\notag\\
 \mathcal L_{\varepsilon}(T,x)
 &:=z^2\left[R_{17/100}P-\frac{42}{25}D^4N^4(T+x)^2\right]
       +\varepsilon D^6N^4>0
 \label{mid:large-sign}\\[-3pt]
 &\hspace{10mm}(0\le T\le T_0,\ -7/10\le x\le21/50),\notag
\end{aligned}$$ where $(T_0,\varepsilon)=(9/10,1/100)$ or $(111/100,1/50)$. Their exact finite verification is given below.

For $m\le1/5$, the lower bound for $\sqrt q$ is $R_{19/100}/D^2$. Hence <a href="#mid:small-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:small-sign">[mid:small-sign]</a> makes the bracket in <a href="#mid:bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:bracket">[mid:bracket]</a> at least $1-(33/10)T^2$. To compare this with the claimed bound, put $y=T^2\le441/10000$. Both sides in the following squared comparison are nonnegative, and $$\left(1-\frac{33}{10}y\right)^2(1+y)-(1-3y)^2
 =y\left(\frac25-\frac{471}{100}y+\frac{1089}{100}y^2\right)\ge0.$$ Indeed $2/5-(471/100)(441/10000)>0$. Consequently $$1-\frac{33}{10}T^2
 \ge\frac{1-3T^2}{\sqrt{1+T^2}}
 =\frac{1-4m^2}{h}.$$ Together with $V=2\sin^2t/h$, this proves the first line of <a href="#mid:pointwise-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:pointwise-bounds">[mid:pointwise-bounds]</a>.

For the two larger ranges the lower bound for $\sqrt q$ is $R_{17/100}/D^2$, and the coefficient of $(T+x)^2$ is at most $42/25$. Since $\sin^2t=z^2/D^2$, Equation <a href="#mid:large-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:large-sign">[mid:large-sign]</a> implies $\Psi/V>-\varepsilon$. This proves the other two lines for $t\ne0$, and their nonstrict form also holds at $t=0$. Notice that division by $N$ has only been used at actual angular points, where <a href="#mid:positive-denominators" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:positive-denominators">[mid:positive-denominators]</a> holds. A larger certification rectangle may contain $N\le0$; its polynomial sign assertion remains valid and is more than is needed here. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The six exact polynomial certificates

<a id="mid:Bernstein-transform"></a>
For completeness, we specify the entire finite calculation behind <a href="#mid:small-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:small-sign">[mid:small-sign]</a>–<a href="#mid:large-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:large-sign">[mid:large-sign]</a>. It uses rational arithmetic and the tensor Bernstein basis described in Appendix <a href="#app:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:arithmetic">9</a>. Explicitly, for $U(A,B)=\sum_{a,b}u_{ab}A^aB^b$ of coordinate degrees at most $(n_1,n_2)$, its coefficients in that basis are $$\label{mid:Bernstein-transform}
 \mathcal B_{ij}(U;n_1,n_2)
 =\sum_{a=0}^{i}\sum_{b=0}^{j}
 u_{ab}\frac{\binom ia\binom jb}{\binom{n_1}a\binom{n_2}b}
 \quad(0\le i\le n_1,\ 0\le j\le n_2).$$ The Bernstein basis functions are nonnegative on the unit square and sum to one. Thus a positive minimum of these coefficients proves positivity throughout that square.

<a id="mid:chart-one"></a>
<a id="mid:chart-two"></a>
For $\mathcal S$, use $(A,B)\in[0,1]^2$, $\sigma\in\{-1,1\}$, and the two charts $$\begin{aligned}
 C_{1,\sigma}(A,B)&=(21\sqrt A/100,\ \sigma B\sqrt A/2),
 \label{mid:chart-one}\\
 C_{2,\sigma}(A,B)&=(21B\sqrt A/100,\ \sigma\sqrt A/2).
 \label{mid:chart-two}
\end{aligned}$$ They cover the required rectangle: choose the first chart when $T/(21/100)\ge |x|/(1/2)$, and the second when the reverse inequality holds. In each chart $\mathcal S\circ C_{j,\sigma}$ has a factor $A$. To make the expansion entirely explicit, if $\mathcal S(T,x)=\sum c_{ij}T^ix^j$, then every nonzero term has $i+j$ even and at least two, and the divided polynomials are <a id="mid:chart-expansion-one"></a><a id="mid:chart-expansion-two"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 U_{1,\sigma}
 &=\sum_{i,j}c_{ij}(21/100)^i(\sigma/2)^j
      A^{(i+j)/2-1}B^j,\label{mid:chart-expansion-one}\\
 U_{2,\sigma}
 &=\sum_{i,j}c_{ij}(21/100)^i(\sigma/2)^j
      A^{(i+j)/2-1}B^i.\label{mid:chart-expansion-two}
\end{aligned}$$ Their degrees are at most $(8,18)$ and $(8,6)$, respectively. The values at $A=0$ are polynomial extensions; after multiplying back by $A\ge0$, the origin is included in the nonstrict sign claim.

<a id="mid:large-map"></a>
<a id="mid:affine-coefficients"></a>
For $\mathcal L_\varepsilon$, subdivide into sixteen equal rectangles. For $j,k\in\{0,1,2,3\}$, use the affine map $$\label{mid:large-map}
 T=\frac{T_0}{4}(j+A),\qquad
 x=-\frac7{10}+\frac7{25}(k+B),\qquad (A,B)\in[0,1]^2.$$ Each resulting polynomial has coordinate degrees at most $(6,20)$. If the untransformed coefficient of $T^ix^j$ is $c_{ij}$, the coefficient of $A^aB^b$ after any affine map $(T,x)=(\ell_T+w_TA,\ell_x+w_xB)$ is exactly $$\label{mid:affine-coefficients}
 \sum_{i\ge a,\ j\ge b}c_{ij}\binom ia\binom jb
 \ell_T^{i-a}w_T^a\ell_x^{j-b}w_x^b.$$ Thus <a href="#mid:polynomial-variables" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:polynomial-variables">[mid:polynomial-variables]</a>–<a href="#mid:R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:R">[mid:R]</a>, <a href="#mid:small-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:small-sign">[mid:small-sign]</a>–<a href="#mid:large-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:large-sign">[mid:large-sign]</a>, and <a href="#mid:Bernstein-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:Bernstein-transform">[mid:Bernstein-transform]</a>–<a href="#mid:affine-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:affine-coefficients">[mid:affine-coefficients]</a> specify every coefficient by a finite rational sum.

Table <a href="#mid:certificate-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mid:certificate-table">2</a> records the minimum coefficients. An entry $M$ in the last column means $\lfloor C\min\mathcal B_{ij}\rfloor=M$, with the minimum also taken over all sixteen boxes in the last two rows. In particular the minimum is at least $M/C>0$. These six integer comparisons are the finite arithmetic part of the pointwise proof; the accompanying exact reconstruction scripts implement the displayed sums.

<div id="mid:certificate-table">

| Polynomial           | Domain map                                                                                                             | Orders   | Scale $C$ | $M$ |
|:---------------------|:-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|:---------|:----------|----:|
| $U_{1,-}$            | <a href="#mid:chart-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:chart-one">[mid:chart-one]</a>, $\sigma=-1$   | $(8,18)$ | $1000$    |  90 |
| $U_{1,+}$            | <a href="#mid:chart-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:chart-one">[mid:chart-one]</a>, $\sigma=1$    | $(8,18)$ | $1000$    |  27 |
| $U_{2,-}$            | <a href="#mid:chart-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:chart-two">[mid:chart-two]</a>, $\sigma=-1$   | $(8,6)$  | $1000$    | 293 |
| $U_{2,+}$            | <a href="#mid:chart-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:chart-two">[mid:chart-two]</a>, $\sigma=1$    | $(8,6)$  | $1000$    | 120 |
| $\mathcal L_{1/100}$ | <a href="#mid:large-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:large-map">[mid:large-map]</a>, $T_0=9/10$    | $(6,20)$ | $10000$   |  53 |
| $\mathcal L_{1/50}$  | <a href="#mid:large-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mid:large-map">[mid:large-map]</a>, $T_0=111/100$ | $(6,20)$ | $10000$   |  26 |

Exact Bernstein certificate minima for the intermediate band.

</div>

<a id="sec:large-bias"></a>
### Large biases

The last bias range admits a direct estimate. We retain the reduction $0\le\rho\le1$. As throughout the proof, $H(t)=\sqrt{1-t^2}$, expectation is uniform on the cube, and $$m=\mathbb Ef,\qquad d=T_\rho f,\qquad h=H(m),\qquad
 b=\mathbb EH(d),\qquad r=\rho^2=1-s^2.$$ All terminating decimals in this section denote exact rational numbers.

<a id="stmt-prop-large-bias"></a>
<div id="prop:large-bias" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18**. *If $f:\{-1,1\}^n\to\{-1,1\}$ has $0.74\le m<1$ and $0.84\le s\le1$, then $$h-b\le1-s.$$*

</div>

Write $U=T_\rho\mathbf1_{\{f=-1\}}=(1-d)/2$, so that $H(d)=2\sqrt U\sqrt{1-U}$. Expanding $\sqrt{1-U}$ will reduce the desired lower bound on $b$ to a lower bound on $\mathbb EU^{1/2}$ and upper bounds on the higher moments $\mathbb EU^{k+1/2}$, $k\ge1$. These are supplied by reverse and forward cube norm inequalities, respectively.

The forward inequality belongs to the classical hypercontractive theory of Bonami and Beckner <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Bonami70](#ref-Bonami70), [Beckner75](#ref-Beckner75)]; the reverse inequality is due to Borell <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Borell82](#ref-Borell82)]. We derive both from the cube logarithmic Sobolev inequality using Gross’s semigroup method <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Gross75](#ref-Gross75)]. The sharp reverse time constant is also given by Mossel, Oleszkiewicz, and Sen <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MOS13](#ref-MOS13), Equation (1.2) and Theorem 1.10] with logarithmic Sobolev constant $2$. The proof below fixes the uniform-bit normalization and the limiting argument for inputs with zeros.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The cube norm inequalities

<a id="lb:dirichlet"></a>
For a positive function $z$ on a finite probability space, write $$\operatorname{Ent}(z)
 =\mathbb E(z\log z)-(\mathbb Ez)\log(\mathbb Ez).$$ Recall the operators from Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>: on the cube, let $\mathbb E_i$ average only coordinate $i$, and set $D_i=I-\mathbb E_i$ and $\mathcal N=\sum_iD_i$. These self-adjoint projections give $$\label{lb:dirichlet}
 \mathbb Ev\mathcal N w=\sum_i\mathbb E(D_iv)(D_iw).$$ In particular, if $v_+,v_-$ and $w_+,w_-$ are the two values on a coordinate edge, its conditional contribution is $$\mathbb E_i[vD_iw]=\frac{(v_+-v_-)(w_+-w_-)}4.$$

<a id="stmt-lb-log-sobolev"></a>
<div id="lb:log-sobolev" class="lemma">

<a id="lb:entropy-bound"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Cube logarithmic Sobolev inequality). *For every positive function $a$ on the uniform cube, $$\label{lb:entropy-bound}
 \operatorname{Ent}(a^2)\le2\mathbb Ea\mathcal Na.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lb-log-sobolev"><summary>Proof of stmt-lb-log-sobolev</summary>

*Proof.* First consider one bit. Both sides are homogeneous of degree two in $a$. After dividing by $\mathbb Ea^2$ and exchanging the two endpoints if necessary, we may suppose that $a_\pm^2=1\pm x$, where $0\le x<1$. The entropy then equals $$J(x)=\frac{(1+x)\log(1+x)+(1-x)\log(1-x)}2,$$ whereas the right side of <a href="#lb:entropy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:entropy-bound">[lb:entropy-bound]</a> equals $$\frac{(a_+-a_-)^2}{2}=1-\sqrt{1-x^2}.$$ Both expressions vanish at zero. Their derivatives satisfy $$J'(x)=\operatorname{arctanh}x\le\frac{x}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}.$$ Indeed, the difference on the right minus the left vanishes at zero and has derivative $(1-x^2)^{-3/2}-(1-x^2)^{-1}\ge0$. Integration proves the one-bit inequality, including its constant $2$.

<a id="lb:tensorization"></a>
For completeness, entropy is convex in its positive input. Its second variation in the direction $w$ is $$\mathbb E\frac{w^2}{z}-\frac{(\mathbb Ew)^2}{\mathbb Ez}\ge0$$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. Let $\operatorname{Ent}_i$ denote entropy on bit $i$ with the other bits fixed. The exact decomposition $$\operatorname{Ent}(z)
 =\mathbb E\operatorname{Ent}_1(z)+\operatorname{Ent}(\mathbb E_1z)$$ and induction on the remaining coordinates imply $$\label{lb:tensorization}
 \operatorname{Ent}(z)\le\sum_i\mathbb E\operatorname{Ent}_i(z).$$ To see the induction step explicitly, for $i\ne1$ convexity gives $\operatorname{Ent}_i(\mathbb E_1z)\le\mathbb E_1\operatorname{Ent}_i(z)$: conditional averaging in coordinate $1$ is a convex combination of the two positive vectors indexed by coordinate $i$. Apply the one-bit inequality to $z=a^2$ in every summand of <a href="#lb:tensorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:tensorization">[lb:tensorization]</a>, and then sum the edge energies using <a href="#lb:dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:dirichlet">[lb:dirichlet]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lb-norm-bounds"></a>
<div id="lb:norm-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="lb:norm-moments"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Forward and reverse cube norm bounds). *Let $u_0\ge0$ be a real function on the cube, $\tau\ge0$, and $r=e^{-2\tau}$. For every $q>0$, $q\ne1$, put $p_q=1+r(q-1)$. Then $$\label{lb:norm-moments}
 \mathbb E(T_{e^{-\tau}}u_0)^q
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\le(\mathbb Eu_0^{p_q})^{q/p_q},&q>1,\\[2pt]
 \displaystyle\ge(\mathbb Eu_0^{p_q})^{q/p_q},&0<q<1.
 \end{cases}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lb-norm-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lb-norm-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* We begin with strictly positive $u_0$. The noise semigroup is $T_{e^{-t}}=e^{-t\mathcal N}$, so $u(t)=T_{e^{-t}}u_0$ satisfies $u'=-\mathcal Nu$. This identity follows, for example, because $\mathcal N$ multiplies a character of degree $j$ by $j$, while noise multiplies it by $e^{-tj}$.

<a id="lb:power-energy"></a>
For positive edge values $A>B$ and any $q>0$, $q\ne1$, Cauchy–Schwarz in the interval $[B,A]$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac4{q^2}(A^{q/2}-B^{q/2})^2
 &=\left(\int_B^A t^{q/2-1}\,dt\right)^2\\
 &\le(A-B)\int_B^A t^{q-2}\,dt
 =\frac{(A-B)(A^{q-1}-B^{q-1})}{q-1}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last quotient is positive also when $0<q<1$. The inequality is symmetric in $A,B$ and holds by continuity when they coincide. Summing it with the edge factor $1/4$ yields $$\label{lb:power-energy}
 \frac{\mathbb Eu^{q-1}\mathcal Nu}{q-1}
 \ge\frac4{q^2}\mathbb Eu^{q/2}\mathcal N(u^{q/2}).$$

To reach a prescribed final exponent $q$, vary the exponent along $$\theta(t)=1+(q-1)e^{2(t-\tau)},\qquad 0\le t\le\tau.$$ This path is positive, stays on the same side of $1$ as $q$, and satisfies $\theta'=2(\theta-1)$, $\theta(0)=p_q$, and $\theta(\tau)=q$. For $M(t)=\mathbb Eu(t)^{\theta(t)}$, direct differentiation gives $$M'=\theta'\mathbb E(u^\theta\log u)
       -\theta\mathbb Eu^{\theta-1}\mathcal Nu.$$ Consequently, with $\|u\|_\theta=(\mathbb Eu^\theta)^{1/\theta}$ also for $0<\theta<1$, <a id="lb:norm-derivative"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{d}{dt}\log\|u\|_\theta
 &=\frac{\theta'}{\theta^2}
      \frac{\operatorname{Ent}(u^\theta)}{M}
      -\frac{\mathbb Eu^{\theta-1}\mathcal Nu}{M}\notag\\
 &=\frac{\theta-1}{M}
   \left[
    \frac2{\theta^2}\operatorname{Ent}(u^\theta)
    -\frac{\mathbb Eu^{\theta-1}\mathcal Nu}{\theta-1}
   \right].\label{lb:norm-derivative}
\end{aligned}$$ Lemma <a href="#lb:log-sobolev" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lb:log-sobolev">19</a>, applied to $u^{\theta/2}$, and <a href="#lb:power-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:power-energy">[lb:power-energy]</a> show that the bracket is nonpositive. Thus the derivative is nonpositive when $q>1$ and nonnegative when $0<q<1$. Integrating, and raising the resulting norm inequality to the positive power $q$, proves <a href="#lb:norm-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:norm-moments">[lb:norm-moments]</a>. For general $u_0\ge0$, apply the result to $u_0+\varepsilon$ and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. The cube is finite, and all exponents involved are positive, so both sides converge continuously. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Reduction to a series of powers

<details id="proof-stmt-lb-norm-bounds-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lb-norm-bounds</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:large-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:large-bias">18</a>.* We first use the norm inequalities to bound a positive series of moments. We then bound its terms and tail uniformly on four intervals of $r$. Finally, exact rational estimates make the resulting bound smaller than $1-s$ on each interval.

The case $s=1$ has $\rho=0$ and $d=m$, hence $h-b=0$. We may therefore assume $0.84\le s<1$, so $0<r\le0.2944$. Write $$p=\Pr(f=-1)=\frac{1-m}{2}\in(0,0.13],\qquad
 U=T_\rho\mathbf1_{\{f=-1\}}=\frac{1-d}{2}.$$ The preceding approximation argument applies to this indicator input. After taking its limit, $0\le U\le1$ and $H(d)=2\sqrt U\sqrt{1-U}$.

<a id="lb:binomial"></a>
<a id="lb:series-bound"></a>
For $0\le z\le1$, the binomial expansion has positive coefficients: $$\label{lb:binomial}
 1-\sqrt{1-z}=\sum_{k\ge1}c_kz^k,\qquad
 c_k=\frac{\binom{2k}{k}}{(2k-1)4^k},\qquad
 c_1=\frac12,\quad
 c_{k+1}=c_k\frac{k-1/2}{k+1}.$$ The usual Taylor expansion gives this identity for $z<1$; positivity and monotone convergence as $z\uparrow1$ give $\sum_{k\ge1}c_k=1$ and the identity at $z=1$. It follows that <a id="lb:series-identity"></a>$$\label{lb:series-identity}
 h-b
 =2(\sqrt p-\mathbb E\sqrt U)
  +2\sum_{k\ge1}c_k\bigl(\mathbb EU^{k+1/2}-p^{k+1/2}\bigr).$$ The interchange with expectation is justified by the nonnegative series for each of the two terms; both series are bounded by $\sum_kc_k=1$. Lemma <a href="#lb:norm-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lb:norm-bounds">20</a> with $q=1/2$ bounds the first expectation from below, and with $q_k=k+1/2$ bounds the other moments from above. Thus $$\label{lb:series-bound}
 h-b\le
 2\bigl[p^{1/2}-p^{1/(2-r)}\bigr]
 +2\sum_{k\ge1}c_k
 \left[p^{q_k/(1+r(q_k-1))}-p^{q_k}\right].$$

Every bracket on the right increases with $p$ on $[0,0.13]$. Indeed, if $1/2\le a<b$ and $p>0$, then $$\frac{d}{dp}(p^a-p^b)\ge0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 p\le(a/b)^{1/(b-a)}.$$ Since $\log(b/a)\le(b-a)/a$, this threshold is at least $e^{-1/a}\ge e^{-2}>0.13$; the last strict inequality follows also from the logarithm bounds established in <a href="#lb:log-brackets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:log-brackets">[lb:log-brackets]</a> below. The exponents in <a href="#lb:series-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:series-bound">[lb:series-bound]</a> have this ordering, since $0<r<1$. We can therefore replace $p$ by $p_*=13/100$ throughout its right side.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Uniform bounds on the four noise intervals

<a id="lb:exponential-envelope"></a>
<a id="lb:log-brackets"></a>
<a id="lb:rational-envelopes"></a>
Put $L=\log(100/13)$ and $L_0=2041/1000$. To replace powers of $p_*$ by rational expressions, define $$\label{lb:exponential-envelope}
 S_N(x)=\sum_{j=0}^N\frac{x^j}{j!},\qquad
 E(z)=\frac1{S_{40}((51/25)z)}\quad(z\ge0).$$ We have the exact rational comparisons $$\label{lb:log-brackets}
 \begin{split}
 S_{15}\!\left(\frac{51}{25}\right)
 +\frac{(51/25)^{16}}{16!\bigl(1-(51/25)/17\bigr)}
 &<\frac{7691}{1000}<\frac{100}{13}\\
 &<\frac{7698}{1000}
 <S_{15}\!\left(\frac{2041}{1000}\right).
 \end{split}$$ For $x=51/25$, the remainder after term $15$ in $e^x$ is at most $x^{16}/[16!(1-x/17)]$: every ratio after the first omitted term is at most $x/17<1$. The truncated sum at $2041/1000$ is less than its full exponential. Therefore <a href="#lb:log-brackets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:log-brackets">[lb:log-brackets]</a> proves $$\label{lb:rational-envelopes}
 2.04<L<2.041=L_0,\qquad
 \sqrt{p_*}<0.361,\qquad p_*^z\le E(z)\quad(z\ge0).$$ The middle inequality follows from $0.361^2>0.13$. For the last one, $S_{40}(2.04z)\le e^{2.04z}\le e^{Lz}$; no upper estimate for the omitted exponential tail is needed there.

Fix one of the four intervals <a id="lb:noise-intervals"></a>$$\label{lb:noise-intervals}
 [\sigma_-,\sigma_+]
 \in\{[0.84,0.88],[0.88,0.92],[0.92,0.96],[0.96,1]\},$$ and let $$\ell=1-\sigma_+^2,\qquad R=1-\sigma_-^2.$$ For $s$ in this interval and $s<1$, we have $0<r\le R$ and $r\ge\ell$. Every expression with $\ell$ in its denominator below is omitted when $\ell=0$.

<a id="lb:B"></a>
First, put $\delta=r/[2(2-r)]$, so that $1/(2-r)=1/2+\delta$. Convexity of $t\mapsto e^{-Lt}$ gives the trapezoid estimate $$\frac{2[p_*^{1/2}-p_*^{1/(2-r)}]}{1-s}
 =\frac{2\sqrt{p_*}L}{1-s}\int_0^\delta e^{-Lt}\,dt
 \le\sqrt{p_*}L\frac{1+s}{1+s^2}
       \frac{1+p_*^\delta}{2}.$$ Here we used $\delta/(1-s)=(1+s)/[2(1+s^2)]$. The function $(1+s)/(1+s^2)$ decreases for $s\ge0.84$, since its derivative has numerator $1-2s-s^2<0$. Also $\delta$ increases with $r$, whence $p_*^\delta\le p_*^{\ell/[2(2-\ell)]}$. Using <a href="#lb:rational-envelopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:rational-envelopes">[lb:rational-envelopes]</a>, define the resulting rational bound $$\label{lb:B}
 B=L_0(0.361)\frac{1+\sigma_-}{1+\sigma_-^2}
       \frac{1+E(\ell/[2(2-\ell)])}{2}.$$

We next bound each remaining difference after division by $r$. For $q=q_k=k+1/2$, put $$z_q(t)=\frac q{1+t(q-1)},\qquad
 a=z_q(R),\qquad b_1=z_q(\ell),\qquad
 F_q(t)=p_*^{z_q(t)}-p_*^q.$$ For every $q\ge3/2$ and $0\le t\le R\le184/625$, all the exponents in use satisfy <a id="lb:exponent-range"></a>$$\label{lb:exponent-range}
 z_q(t)\ge\frac{3/2}{1+(184/625)/2}
 =\frac{625}{478}>1.3.$$ To verify the first inequality, $z_q(t)$ decreases in $t$ and increases in $q$, because $t<1$. By $L>2.04$, the functions $z p_*^z$ and $z^2p_*^z$ strictly decrease for $z\ge1.3$: their derivative signs are those of $1-Lz$ and $2-Lz$, respectively.

There are three useful estimates for $F_q(r)/r$.

1.  Differentiation gives $$F_q'(t)=L(1-1/q)z_q(t)^2p_*^{z_q(t)}.$$ Since $F_q(0)=0$ and $z_q(t)\ge a$ for $0\le t\le r$, integration and the decreasing property just proved give $$\frac{F_q(r)}r\le L_0(1-1/q)a^2E(a).$$

2.  If $\ell>0$, discarding the subtracted power and using $r\ge\ell$ gives the direct estimate $$\frac{F_q(r)}r\le\frac{p_*^{z_q(r)}}{\ell}
     \le\frac{E(a)}\ell.$$

3.  Integration in the exponent, followed by the trapezoid bound, gives, with $z=z_q(r)$, $$\begin{aligned}
     \frac{F_q(r)}r
     &=\frac Lr\int_z^q p_*^t\,dt
     \le\frac{L(q-z)}{2r}(p_*^z+p_*^q)\\
     &=\frac{L(q-1)}2\bigl[zp_*^z+zp_*^q\bigr]
     \le\frac{L_0(q-1)}2\bigl[aE(a)+b_1E(q)\bigr].
    \end{aligned}$$ Here $q-z=r(q-1)z$, the first summand uses the decrease of $zp_*^z$ and $z\ge a$, and the second uses $z\le b_1$.

<a id="lb:Bk"></a>
It follows that, for $1\le k\le10$, $$\label{lb:Bk}
 \begin{split}
 \frac{F_{q_k}(r)}r\le B_k:=\min\biggl\{&
 L_0(1-1/q_k)a^2E(a),\;
 \frac{E(a)}\ell,\\
 &\frac{L_0(q_k-1)}2[aE(a)+b_1E(q_k)]
 \biggr\}.
 \end{split}$$

<a id="lb:Btail"></a>
For the tail, set $$\label{lb:Btail}
 a_{11}=\frac{23/2}{1+R(21/2)},\qquad
 B'=\min\left\{L_0a_{11}^2E(a_{11}),\;
                   \frac{E(a_{11})}\ell\right\}.$$ For $k\ge11$, $z_{q_k}(R)\ge a_{11}$, again by its increase with $q$. The first estimate for $F_q(r)/r$, with $1-1/q\le1$, is therefore bounded by $L_0a_{11}^2E(a_{11})$, using the decrease of $z^2p_*^z$ before replacing the power by $E$. The direct estimate is bounded by $E(a_{11})/\ell$ in the same way. This proves $F_{q_k}(r)/r\le B'$ for every $k\ge11$. In the interval ending at $1$, only the first of these tail estimates is used; no division by zero occurs.

<a id="lb:finite-tail-sums"></a>
<a id="lb:final-reduction"></a>
Finally, define $$\label{lb:finite-tail-sums}
 \begin{split}
 S&=\sum_{k=1}^{10}c_k B_k,\qquad
 T=\left(1-\sum_{k=1}^{10}c_k\right)B',\\
 C&=B+2(1+\sigma_+)(S+T).
 \end{split}$$ Since $r/(1-s)=1+s\le1+\sigma_+$, Equations <a href="#lb:series-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:series-bound">[lb:series-bound]</a>, <a href="#lb:B" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:B">[lb:B]</a>, <a href="#lb:Bk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:Bk">[lb:Bk]</a>, and <a href="#lb:Btail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:Btail">[lb:Btail]</a> imply $$\label{lb:final-reduction}
 \frac{h-b}{1-s}\le C.$$

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Exact rational evaluation

Here are the coefficient data and arithmetic recipe for the final check. The first ten coefficients from <a href="#lb:binomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:binomial">[lb:binomial]</a>, and their remaining mass, are exactly <a id="lb:coefficient-data"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 (c_1,\ldots,c_{10})
 &=\left(\frac12,\frac18,\frac1{16},\frac5{128},\frac7{256},
 \frac{21}{1024},\frac{33}{2048},\frac{429}{32768},
 \frac{715}{65536},\frac{2431}{262144}\right),\notag\\
 1-\sum_{k=1}^{10}c_k&=\frac{46189}{262144}.
 \label{lb:coefficient-data}
\end{aligned}$$ For rational $x$, compute $S_N(x)$ entirely rationally by starting $t_0=1$ and successively taking $t_j=t_{j-1}x/j$, then summing $t_0+\cdots+t_N$. This evaluates the two order-$15$ comparisons in <a href="#lb:log-brackets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:log-brackets">[lb:log-brackets]</a> and every order-$40$ expression in <a href="#lb:exponential-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:exponential-envelope">[lb:exponential-envelope]</a>. Substitution in <a href="#lb:B" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:B">[lb:B]</a>–<a href="#lb:finite-tail-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:finite-tail-sums">[lb:finite-tail-sums]</a>, using only rational multiplication, addition, division, and comparison, gives Table <a href="#lb:arithmetic-table" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lb:arithmetic-table">3</a>. A minimum in <a href="#lb:Bk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:Bk">[lb:Bk]</a> or <a href="#lb:Btail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:Btail">[lb:Btail]</a> is found by comparing its rational candidates; the candidate with denominator $\ell$ is excluded when $\ell=0$.

<a id="lb:rounded-total"></a>
In the table, $\overline B=\lceil10^9 B\rceil$, $\overline S=\lceil10^9 S\rceil$, and $\overline T=\lceil10^9 T\rceil$ are *integers*. Thus the table also supplies the simpler rational upper bound $$\label{lb:rounded-total}
 C\le\frac{\overline B+
                2(1+\sigma_+)(\overline S+\overline T)}{10^9}.$$ For an exact fraction $x=A/D$ with positive denominator, its required ceiling is $\lceil10^9A/D\rceil$, evaluated by integer division. The recipe therefore involves no floating-point operations, including in the rounding step. The executable companion `verification/large_bias.py` checks these coefficient data, the logarithm brackets, each table entry, and the final comparisons.

<div id="lb:arithmetic-table">

| $[\sigma_-,\sigma_+]$ |  $\ell$   |    $R$    | $\overline B$ | $\overline S$ | $\overline T$ | upper bound |
|:---------------------:|:---------:|:---------:|--------------:|--------------:|--------------:|:-----------:|
|     $[0.84,0.88]$     | $141/625$ | $184/625$ |   $746521967$ |    $60049597$ |     $2525240$ |   $0.982$   |
|     $[0.88,0.92]$     | $96/625$  | $141/625$ |   $748896408$ |    $56019174$ |     $1084482$ |   $0.969$   |
|     $[0.92,0.96]$     | $49/625$  | $96/625$  |   $750555827$ |    $51401364$ |      $283275$ |   $0.954$   |
|      $[0.96,1]$       |    $0$    | $49/625$  |   $751524751$ |    $46967364$ |       $36923$ |   $0.940$   |

Exact integer ceilings for the rational bounds. The expression in <a href="#lb:rounded-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:rounded-total">[lb:rounded-total]</a> is strictly below the final column in every row.

</div>

For explicit final arithmetic, the four right sides of <a href="#lb:rounded-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:rounded-total">[lb:rounded-total]</a> are, in order, $$\begin{gathered}
 \frac{24545083853}{25000000000}<\frac{982}{1000},\qquad
 \frac{3025545147}{3125000000}<\frac{969}{1000},\\
 \frac{23828990297}{25000000000}<\frac{954}{1000},\qquad
 \frac{939541899}{1000000000}<\frac{940}{1000}.
\end{gathered}$$ Every displayed upper bound is strictly below $1$. The four intervals cover $0.84\le s<1$, so <a href="#lb:final-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lb:final-reduction">[lb:final-reduction]</a> proves $h-b\le1-s$ throughout the claimed range. The already treated endpoint $s=1$ completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:completion"></a>
### Completion of the proof

<details id="proof-stmt-lb-norm-bounds-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lb-norm-bounds</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The endpoint and symmetry reductions in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> reduce the problem to $0<\rho<1$ and a nonconstant function. Theorem <a href="#thm:induction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:induction">2</a> proves Equation <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> when $0<s\le .84$. For larger $s$, replace $f$ by $-f$ if necessary so that $m\ge0$. Proposition <a href="#prop:large-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:large-bias">18</a> gives the desired inequality whenever $m\ge .74$. If $m<.74$ and the inequality were ever to fail, Lemma <a href="#lem:crossing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:crossing">7</a> would provide a parameter satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crossing">[eq:crossing]</a>. At that parameter, either $m\le r^2/2$ or $m\ge r^2/2$. Propositions <a href="#prop:central" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:central">10</a> and <a href="#prop:intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:intermediate">16</a> exclude these possibilities unless $f$ is a signed coordinate, for which equality is already known. This proves the theorem for every $n,f,\rho$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The information-theoretic consequence

For $0\le p\le1$, write $$h_2(p)=-p\log_2p-(1-p)\log_2(1-p),$$ with $0\log_2 0=0$. Mutual information in the next statement is measured in bits. The argument is the implication of Anantharam, Bogdanov, Chakrabarti, Jayram, and Nair <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[ABCJN](#ref-ABCJN), Proposition 1 and Lemma 1], reproduced here in our normalization. It applies to Boolean summaries of a uniform input, including summaries with nonzero mean.

<a id="stmt-cor-ck"></a>
<div id="cor:ck" class="corollary">

**Corollary 21** (Courtade–Kumar bound). *Let $X$ be uniform on $\{-1,1\}^n$, let $f$ be Boolean, and let $Y_i=X_iZ_i$, where the $Z_i$ are independent of $X$ and each other, with $\Pr(Z_i=-1)=\varepsilon\in[0,1/2]$. Then $$I(f(X);Y)\le1-h_2(\varepsilon).$$ Signed coordinates attain equality.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-ck"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-ck</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\rho=1-2\varepsilon$, $d=T_\rho f$, $m=\mathbb Ef$, $h=H(m)$, $b=\mathbb EH(d)$, and $s=H(\rho)$. Define $$\varphi(t)=h_2\left(\frac{1-\sqrt{1-t^2}}2\right),\qquad 0\le t\le1.$$ This function is increasing and convex. In fact, for $0<t<1$, writing $v=\sqrt{1-t^2}$ gives $$\varphi'(t)=\frac{t}{2v\log 2}\log\frac{1+v}{1-v}\ge0,
 \qquad
 \varphi''(t)=\frac{\log((1+v)/(1-v))-2v}{2v^3\log 2}\ge0.$$ The last numerator is nonnegative because its derivative with respect to $v$ is $2v^2/(1-v^2)\ge0$ and its value at zero is zero. Continuity extends monotonicity and convexity to $[0,1]$.

The symmetric noise kernel gives $\Pr(f(X)=1\mid Y)=(1+d(Y))/2$. The identity $h_2((1+u)/2)=\varphi(H(u))$, valid for every $u\in[-1,1]$, and Jensen’s inequality therefore imply $$I(f(X);Y)=\varphi(h)-\mathbb E\varphi(H(d))
 \le\varphi(h)-\varphi(b).$$ Concavity of $H$ gives $b\le h$, and Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives $\delta:=h-b\le1-s$. For a convex function its increments over intervals of fixed length are increasing with the left endpoint. Since $h\le1$, $$\varphi(h)-\varphi(b)
 \le\varphi(1)-\varphi(1-\delta)
 \le\varphi(1)-\varphi(s)
 =1-h_2(\varepsilon).$$ The same reasoning includes $\delta=0$ and the noise endpoints by continuity. A signed coordinate is a uniform bit observed through a binary symmetric channel, whose mutual information is $1-h_2(\varepsilon)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:arithmetic"></a>
### Exact arithmetic conventions

All constants in the certificates are rational; a terminating decimal denotes the corresponding rational number exactly. This appendix explains the common sign-certification rules. The individual polynomials, parameter maps, and subdivision orders are specified where they are used.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Bernstein coefficients

<a id="eq:bernstein-expansion"></a>
We use the standard enclosure supplied by the Bernstein form of a polynomial; see Cargo and Shisha <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[CargoShisha66](#ref-CargoShisha66), Section 3] for the univariate coefficient identity and range bound. We include the tensor-product formula and its proof to make the rational checks explicit. Let $P(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^N\sum_{\ell=0}^K a_{k\ell}x^ky^\ell$. On the unit square its Bernstein expansion is $$\label{eq:bernstein-expansion}
P(x,y)=\sum_{i=0}^N\sum_{j=0}^K b_{ij}
\binom Ni x^i(1-x)^{N-i}\binom Kj y^j(1-y)^{K-j},$$ where <a id="eq:bernstein-coefficients"></a>$$\label{eq:bernstein-coefficients}
b_{ij}=
\sum_{\substack{0\le k\le i\\0\le\ell\le j}}
a_{k\ell}
\frac{\binom ik}{\binom Nk}
\frac{\binom j\ell}{\binom K\ell}.$$ Indeed, $$x^k=\sum_{i=k}^N
\frac{\binom ik}{\binom Nk}\binom Ni x^i(1-x)^{N-i},$$ by the binomial theorem after factoring out $x^k$; multiplying two such identities proves the formula. The basis functions in Equation <a href="#eq:bernstein-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bernstein-expansion">[eq:bernstein-expansion]</a> are nonnegative and sum to one. Therefore <a id="eq:bernstein-lower"></a>$$\label{eq:bernstein-lower}
P(x,y)\ge\min_{i,j}b_{ij}\qquad(0\le x,y\le1).$$ The univariate version is obtained by omitting $y$.

For a rational rectangle, first substitute the affine maps from the unit square. For a polynomial obtained after a rational or algebraic change of variables, the displayed denominator is cleared only where it is positive, as verified in the corresponding argument. A positive common scale may be applied to all coefficients, making every calculation integral. If a table reports $\lfloor C\min b_{ij}\rfloor$ for a stated $C>0$, a positive table entry is an exact strict sign certificate.

Subintervals can be handled either by direct substitution or by de Casteljau subdivision. For subdivision at $1/2$, start from a Bernstein row $(b_0,\ldots,b_N)$, repeatedly replace neighboring entries by their arithmetic mean, and take the left and right outer diagonals as the Bernstein rows on the two halves. This rule follows by substituting $x=t/2$ and $x=(1+t)/2$ into the basis expansion. It is exact over the rationals; a common power of two clears all new denominators. For rectangles, apply the rule separately in each coordinate.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Intervals and derivative enclosures

An interval $[a,b]$ encloses a real quantity if $a\le x\le b$. Addition, subtraction, multiplication, and reciprocal use the endpoint extrema, with a reciprocal taken only when zero is excluded. Square roots are taken only on nonnegative intervals. For a positive integer scale $Q$, rounding a lower endpoint down to a multiple of $1/Q$ and an upper endpoint up preserves enclosure. Scaled square roots reduce to integer square-root bounds: for an integer $A\ge0$, $$\lfloor\sqrt A\rfloor^2\le A<
(\lfloor\sqrt A\rfloor+1)^2.$$ Consequently no floating-point operation is needed for an interval certificate.

A derivative enclosure supplies a second bound on a box. If a function has center enclosure $I_0$, coordinate half-widths $h_i$, and derivative magnitude bounds $M_i$, then <a id="eq:mean-value-box"></a>$$\label{eq:mean-value-box}
P(\text{box})\subseteq
I_0+\left[-\sum_i h_iM_i,\;\sum_i h_iM_i\right].$$ This follows by integrating the derivative along a segment from the center. The same statement holds with bounded limiting derivatives at a boundary whenever the resulting Lipschitz estimate extends continuously. At a square-root singularity the direct interval enclosure is used instead. The pair certificate in Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a> specifies which bounds are used and how uncertified boxes are subdivided. An undefined local interval operation causes further subdivision, never acceptance.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Supplementary sources

The complete certificate sources accompany the paper in the directory `verification/`. They are organized as follows.

<div class="center">

| Source                     | Certified calculations                                                                                               |
|:---------------------------|:---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| `interval_certificate.cpp` | The three pair cases in Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a>. |
| `profile_certificate.py`   | The polynomial profiles, their branch inequalities, and the three-bit base cases.                                    |
| `central_band.py`          | The central-band polynomial signs and rational constant bounds.                                                      |
| `intermediate_bias.py`     | The six intermediate-bias Bernstein bounds.                                                                          |
| `large_bias.py`            | The four norm-estimate bounds, including the series tail.                                                            |
| `profile_formula_audit.py` | An independent coefficient reconstruction of the profile formulas and printed data.                                  |

</div>

The accompanying README gives the compiler and Python requirements and a single command to reproduce the calculations. The C++ source uses outward-rounded integer intervals with signed extended 128-bit intermediates; Appendix <a href="#app:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:scalar">10</a> gives the relevant bounds. The principal Python profile certificate uses arbitrary-precision integer arithmetic. The symbolic reconstruction scripts use exact rational coefficients. The arguments proving the enclosure rules, the complete domain coverage, and the passage from the scalar inequalities to the dimension-free theorem are part of the paper.

<a id="app:scalar"></a>
### The scalar certificates

This appendix proves the scalar assertions of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar">5</a>. We first verify the two-point inequality in three regions. The normalized checks near the two endpoints also prove concavity of $G$, which is then used in the third region. We next verify the polynomial conditions that make the reflected profile reach the target and close the dimension induction. Finally, we prove the initial estimate on at most three coordinates. In the profile and base checks the noise parameter varies over a whole interval; endpoint bounds and interval enclosures, respectively, will control that variation. All decimal numbers in this appendix are exact terminating decimals. The seven choices of $(L,U,V,\alpha,\Lambda,\mu)$ and the bounds $(r_0,r_1)$ are those of Table <a href="#ind:tab:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:tab:parameters">1</a>. A parameter choice is fixed throughout each calculation. At an endpoint shared by two ranges one uses an entire row, including its profile, consistently. The finite computations below involve only fixed polynomials and functions of at most three real variables; there is no dimension-dependent search. Their arithmetic recursions and all numerical input data are specified here. The accompanying files `verification/interval_certificate.cpp` and `verification/profile_certificate.py` implement these recursions.

<a id="sc:pair-reduction"></a>
#### Reduction of the pair inequality

Write $\delta=(1-\rho)/2$, so $s^2=4\delta(1-\delta)$. The table bounds $r_0^2\leq\delta\leq r_1^2$ follow by comparing $$4r_0^2(1-r_0^2)\leq s_{\rm lo}^2,
 \qquad 4r_1^2(1-r_1^2)\geq s_{\rm hi}^2,
 \qquad r_1^2<\tfrac12.$$ These are rational comparisons, and $a\mapsto4a(1-a)$ is increasing on $[0,1/2]$. We prove the pair assertion for $s>0$ and $p_0<p_1$; when $p_0=p_1$, both its deficit and its shear are zero.

<a id="sc:KW"></a>
<a id="sc:Delta"></a>
For a smooth function $F$, use divided differences $$F[a,b]=\frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a},\qquad
 F[a,b,c]=\frac{F[b,c]-F[a,b]}{c-a},$$ with repeated arguments interpreted by continuous extension whenever the function is smooth there. Put $z=p_1-p_0$, $p_t=p_0+tz$, and $$\label{sc:KW}
 K=-\frac{G[p_0,p_\delta,p_1]+G[p_0,p_{1-\delta},p_1]}8,
 \qquad W=1-\frac{G[p_0,p_1]}{4L}.$$ The interpolation identity $$G(p_t)-(1-t)G(p_0)-tG(p_1)
   =-t(1-t)z^2G[p_0,p_t,p_1]$$ gives $D=s^2z^2K$. Moreover, the shear in Equation <a href="#ind:eq:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:pair">[ind:eq:pair]</a> is $zW$. Thus the strict inequality $$\label{sc:Delta}
 \Delta:=K(S+\Lambda D)-\mu W^2>0$$ implies its required nonstrict inequality. We will also prove $D\geq0$.

<a id="sc:representations"></a>
The following change of variable gives smooth normalized expressions near the endpoints, where derivatives of $G$ otherwise interfere with interval evaluation. For $0\leq y<1$, put $q=\sqrt{1-y^2}$, $h=2qy$, and $e=(1+h\lambda(h))^{-1}$, and define <a id="sc:NRM"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 N(y)&=4q(y+q\lambda(h))e,\nonumber\\
 R(y)&=4q^2(q+y\lambda(h))e,\label{sc:NRM}\\
 M(y)&=4\bigl\{Ly+q[2L^2-1-2Uq^2
             +h(2LU-2Vq^2+2LVh)]\bigr\}e.\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Direct substitution into Equation <a href="#ind:eq:G" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:G">[ind:eq:G]</a>, or multiplication by $1+h\lambda(h)$, proves $$\label{sc:representations}
 G(y^2)=y^2N(y)=4Ly^2-y^3M(y),
 \qquad G(1-y^2)=yR(y).$$ For every parameter row, $U<0$, $V>0$, and $\lambda(h)\geq L+U\geq.488$ on $[0,1]$. Consequently all the denominators just used are positive, and $N,R,M$ are smooth at the arguments used below, which stay strictly below $1$. Also $N(y),R(y)>0$ for $0\leq y<1$.

The three expressions separate different endpoint behaviors. $N$ removes the factor $p$ from $G(p)$ at $p=0$, while $R$ removes the factor $\sqrt{1-p}$ at $p=1$. The second representation of $G(y^2)$ shows that the right derivative is $G'(0)=4L$: $M$ measures the remainder after subtracting this tangent. Consequently the shear $z-(G(p_1)-G(p_0))/(4L)$ vanishes for the linear function $G(p)=4Lp$. Using $M$ in the first endpoint region preserves this cancellation before any interval bounds are taken; using $R$ in the second removes the square-root factor there.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### Two endpoint regions.

<a id="sc:uwt"></a>
Case 1 is $$p_0=v^2u^2,\quad p_1=v^2,\qquad
 (u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,.8].$$ Case 2 is $$p_0=1-v^2,\quad p_1=1-v^2u^2,\qquad
 (u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,.8].$$ In both cases introduce $j\in[0,r_1]$ and set $$\label{sc:uwt}
 d=j(2u+(1-u)j),\quad w=u+(1-u)j,\quad
 t=\sqrt{1-(1-u)d},\quad \delta=\frac d{1+u},$$ retaining only parameters with $r_0^2\leq\delta\leq r_1^2$. Indeed $$\delta=j^2+\frac{2u}{1+u}j(1-j),\quad
 \partial_u\delta=\frac{2j(1-j)}{(1+u)^2},\quad
 \partial_j\delta=\frac{2(u+(1-u)j)}{1+u}.$$ It increases in both variables and, for each fixed $u$, runs continuously from $0$ to at least $r_1^2$. Hence every required $\delta$ is represented. The identities $$w^2=u^2+\delta(1-u^2),\qquad
 t^2=1-\delta(1-u^2)$$ identify the two noisy mixture points (their order is reversed in Case 2). They also give $u\leq w,t\leq1$.

Let $m^i$, for $i=w,t$, be the divided-difference tuple of the function $a\mapsto M(va)$ in Case 1 and $a\mapsto R(va)$ in Case 2 at the three nodes $(u,i,1)$. Its seven entries are indexed by $0,1,2,01,02,12,012$; for example, $m^i_{02}$ is the first divided difference on the endpoint nodes. The common endpoint entries are written $m_0,m_2,m_{02}$. Set $$\mathcal E(a,b)=\frac{a^2}{a+b}\quad(0\leq a\leq b),\qquad
 \mathcal E(0,0)=0.$$

<a id="sc:case1-data"></a>
<a id="sc:case1"></a>
In Case 1 define $$\begin{aligned}
 F&=\left(1+\frac{u^2}{1+u}\right)m_2
                  +\frac{u^3}{1+u}m_{02},\nonumber\\
 S_0&=\alpha(4L-vm_2)
               +(1-\alpha)u^2(4L-vu m_0),\nonumber\\
 P_i&=\frac{(1-\mathcal E(u,i)/(1+u))m_2
            +(i+\mathcal E(u,i))m^i_{12}
            +\mathcal E(u,i)u(m^i_{012}-m_{02}/(1+u))}{1+i},
       \quad i=w,t,\label{sc:case1-data}\\
 Z&=\frac{P_w+P_t}{8}.\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ The finite sign check in Subsection <a href="#sc:interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:interval">10.2</a> proves $$\label{sc:case1}
 ZS_0+4\Lambda d(1-u)(1-w^2)vZ^2
                     -\frac{\mu vF^2}{(4L)^2}>0.$$ Here the endpoint first difference of $b^{3/2}M(v\sqrt b)$ on $(u^2,1)$ is $F$, and its second difference on $(u^2,i^2,1)$ is $P_i$. For clarity, the identities behind the latter statement are $$\begin{aligned}
 (b^{3/2})[u^2,i^2]&=i+\mathcal E(u,i),\\
 (b^{3/2})[u^2,i^2,1]&=
           \frac{1-\mathcal E(u,i)/(1+u)}{1+i},\\
 (M(v\sqrt b))[u^2,i^2,1]&=
           \frac{m^i_{012}-m_{02}/(1+u)}{(u+i)(1+i)}.
\end{aligned}$$ They follow by factoring differences of squares and applying the product rule for divided differences stated below. The final formula in Equation <a href="#sc:case1-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:case1-data">[sc:case1-data]</a> has a continuous extension at the corner $u=i=0$. Using Equation <a href="#sc:representations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:representations">[sc:representations]</a> gives, for a nondegenerate pair, $$K=Z/v,\qquad W=vF/(4L),\qquad S=v^2S_0,
 \qquad s^2(1-u^2)^2=4d(1-u)(1-w^2).$$ The left side of Equation <a href="#sc:case1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:case1">[sc:case1]</a> is therefore $\Delta/v$. Since $v>0$ for an actual pair, Equation <a href="#sc:case1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:case1">[sc:case1]</a> implies Equation <a href="#sc:Delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:Delta">[sc:Delta]</a> in Case 1.

<a id="sc:case2"></a>
In Case 2 define <a id="sc:case2-data"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F&=\frac{m_2+u m_{02}}{1+u},\nonumber\\
 Z&=\frac18\left[
 \frac{F-m^w_{12}-u m^w_{012}}{1+w}
 +\frac{u+w}{u+t}\frac{F-m^t_{12}-u m^t_{012}}{1+t}
 \right],\label{sc:case2-data}\\
 S_1&=(\alpha-\Lambda/2)u m_0
       +(1-\alpha-\Lambda/2)m_2
       +(\Lambda/2)(w m^w_1+t m^t_1).\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ The second finite sign check is $$\label{sc:case2}
 ZS_1-\mu(u+w)\left(v+\frac F{4L}\right)^2>0.$$ Applying the same difference-of-squares identities to $\sqrt b R(v\sqrt b)$ now gives $$Kv^3(u+w)=Z,\quad W=1+F/(4Lv),\quad S+\Lambda D=vS_1.$$ Thus the left side of Equation <a href="#sc:case2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:case2">[sc:case2]</a> equals $v^2(u+w)\Delta$. For a required pair $v>0$; and $u+w=0$ would force $u=j=0$ and $\delta=0$, excluded here. This proves Equation <a href="#sc:Delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:Delta">[sc:Delta]</a> in Case 2.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Strict concavity, before the third region.

The preceding sign checks also hold at repeated nodes, by their smooth normalized formulas. Fix $u=1$ and any permitted $j=\delta>0$. Such a value lies in the certified interval $[0,r_1]$, since $\delta\le r_1^2<r_1$. Then $w=t=1$. In Case 1, $S_0=N(v)>0$ and the $Z^2$ term of Equation <a href="#sc:case1" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:case1">[sc:case1]</a> vanishes, so that inequality directly forces $Z>0$. In Case 2, $S_1=R(v)>0$, and Equation <a href="#sc:case2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:case2">[sc:case2]</a> likewise directly forces $Z>0$. No prior sign of $K$ is used in either inference. For $v>0$ the identities above imply $K=-G''(p_0)/8>0$ on, respectively, $(0,.64]$ and $[.36,1)$. These intervals cover $(0,1)$; hence $G$ is strictly concave there. Since $G$ is continuous on $[0,1]$, its interpolation deficit is strictly positive for any distinct endpoint pair and any interpolation weight in $(0,1)$, including pairs containing $0$ or $1$. It follows that $D>0$ and $K>0$ for every actual pair under consideration. This proves the separate assertion $D\geq0$ and supplies the sign of $K$ needed next.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### The remaining region.

<a id="sc:Y"></a>
<a id="sc:case3"></a>
Cases 1 and 2 cover $p_1\leq.64$ and $p_0\geq.36$. Any pair outside them is in Case 3: $$p_0=l^2,\quad p_1=1-u^2,\qquad
 (l,u,r)\in[0,.6]^2\times[r_0,r_1],\qquad \delta=r^2.$$ Put <a id="sc:case3-data"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 z&=1-l^2-u^2,\quad b=r^2z,\quad
 l'=\sqrt{l^2+b},\quad u'=\sqrt{u^2+b},\nonumber\\
 P&=N(l')+\mathcal E(l,l')N[l,l'],\qquad
 Q=R(u')+uR[u,u'],\label{sc:case3-data}\\
 Z&=\frac{Q+(u+u')P}{8(1-r^2)z},\qquad
 W=1-\frac{uR(u)-l^2N(l)}{4Lz}.\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Here $z\geq.28$, so these denominators stay positive. The endpoint increments in $G$ are $bP$ and $(u'-u)Q$, respectively. As $b=(u+u')(u'-u)$, this proves $Z=(u+u')K$ and identifies $W$ with Equation <a href="#sc:KW" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:KW">[sc:KW]</a>. Set $$\begin{aligned}
 Y_0={}&Z\bigl[(\alpha-\Lambda/2)uR(u)
       +(\Lambda/2)u'R(u')+(1-\alpha)l^2N(l)
       +(\Lambda/2)bP\bigr]-\mu(u+u')W^2,\nonumber\\
 Y_1={}&Z\bigl[(\alpha/2)R(u)
                   +(\Lambda/2)(u'-u)P\bigr]-\mu W^2,\label{sc:Y}\\
 Y_2={}&Z\bigl[(\Lambda/2)Q
                   +(\Lambda/2)(u'-u)P\bigr]-\mu W^2.\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ The third finite check proves the disjunction $$\label{sc:case3}
 Y_0>0\quad\hbox{or}\quad (Y_1>0\ \hbox{and}\ Y_2>0).$$ The first alternative suffices because $Y_0=(u+u')\Delta$. In the second alternative take the convex combination of the brackets in $Y_1,Y_2$ with weights $2u/(u+u')$ and $(u'-u)/(u+u')$. It is $$\frac{\alpha uR(u)+(\Lambda/2)(u'-u)Q+(\Lambda/2)bP}{u+u'}
 =\frac{S+\Lambda D-(1-\alpha)l^2N(l)}{u+u'}
 \leq\frac{S+\Lambda D}{u+u'}.$$ For $s>0$ we have $u+u'>0$, and the concavity already proved gives $Z=(u+u')K>0$. Therefore the second alternative also implies Equation <a href="#sc:Delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:Delta">[sc:Delta]</a>. The three regions prove Equation <a href="#ind:eq:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:eq:pair">[ind:eq:pair]</a>, subject only to the following explicit finite sign checks.

<a id="sc:interval"></a>
#### Interval arithmetic for the three pair regions

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### Enclosures and derivatives.

All intervals may have rational endpoints. The particular certificate rounds outwards after every operation to multiples of $Q_*^{-1}$, where $Q_*=10^{12}$. Store an interval as $[a/Q_*,b/Q_*]$ with integers $a\leq b$. Addition and subtraction use their ordinary endpoint rules. For multiplication, take the least and greatest of the four endpoint products and round their quotients by $Q_*$ down and up. For a positive interval its reciprocal endpoints are $$Q_*^{-1}\left\lfloor\frac{Q_*^2}{b}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad Q_*^{-1}\left\lceil\frac{Q_*^2}{a}\right\rceil.$$ For a nonnegative interval the square-root endpoints are $Q_*^{-1}\lfloor\sqrt{aQ_*}\rfloor$ and $Q_*^{-1}\lceil\sqrt{bQ_*}\rceil$; integer square comparison computes these exactly. Failed positivity or domain conditions never certify a box. A power with a nonnegative small integer exponent is evaluated by repeated interval multiplication, including for squares; this may overestimate but remains an enclosure.

An interval expression may be augmented by its three first derivatives. Use the sum and product rules, and $$\partial_i(X^{-1})=-X^{-2}\partial_iX,\qquad
 \partial_i\sqrt X=\frac{\partial_iX}{2\sqrt X}.$$ The latter derivative is used only when the lower bound for the root is strictly positive. In Case 3 the radicands defining $l',u'$ are known nonnegative on the whole box; direct *value* evaluation may therefore replace a negative interval lower endpoint by zero. Derivative evaluation does not use this clipping and fails at an uncertified root singularity.

<a id="sc:E-derivative"></a>
The function $\mathcal E(a,b)$ has a special rule on $0\leq a\leq b$. Enclose $h=a/(a+b)$ by $[0,1/2]$, intersecting with direct interval division if $a+b$ has a positive lower bound. Its value is enclosed by $ah$, and its differential by $$\label{sc:E-derivative}
 d\mathcal E=h(2-h)\,da-h^2\,db.$$ At $(0,0)$ the function is continuous and Lipschitz on this cone, so the same difference bounds hold by limits from its interior; differentiability at that point is not assumed. The required order relations $u\leq w,t$ and $l\leq l'$ hold throughout each parameter box, not just on its $\delta$-restricted subset. Thus these enclosures apply on the line segments used in the mean-value estimate below.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### Tuples without division by node distances.

<a id="sc:tuple-product"></a>
For three nodes let $a$ denote the tuple of values and divided differences of a function. Addition is componentwise. For $c=ab$, the product rule is $$\label{sc:tuple-product}
 c_p=a_pb_p,\quad
 c_{pq}=a_pb_{pq}+a_{pq}b_q\ (p<q),\quad
 c_{012}=a_0b_{012}+a_{01}b_{12}+a_{012}b_2.$$ For $a=c^{-1}$ use <a id="sc:tuple-reciprocal"></a>$$\label{sc:tuple-reciprocal}
 a_p=1/c_p,\quad a_{pq}=-c_{pq}a_pa_q,\quad
 a_{012}=-a_0(c_{012}a_2+c_{01}a_{12});$$ for $a=\sqrt c$ use <a id="sc:tuple-root"></a>$$\label{sc:tuple-root}
 a_p=\sqrt{c_p},\quad a_{pq}=\frac{c_{pq}}{a_p+a_q},\quad
 a_{012}=\frac{c_{012}-a_{01}a_{12}}{a_0+a_2}.$$ The last two formulas solve $ac=1$ and $a^2=c$ using Equation <a href="#sc:tuple-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:tuple-product">[sc:tuple-product]</a>.
